AMDG

Resumo

Há tempo que é sabido que o conceito clássico de radiação não é covariante: para partículas uniformemente aceleradas, ele depende do estado de movimento do
observador em relação à partícula que a emite. Além disso, a literatura recente no campo da Teoria Quântica de Campos em Espaços-Tempos Curvos sugere uma
conexão profunda entre o bremsstrahlung e o efeito Unruh, onde partículas de energia nula de Rindler desempenham um papel crucial na construção do conteúdo de
radiação dos sistemas; já que este é um conceito pouco familiar, parece perturbador usar partículas sem energia. Esta tese estuda tal conexão, tanto na
perspectiva clássica quanto quântica, mostrando como o efeito Unruh quântico é responsável pela radiação clássica detectada nos casos eletromagnético e
gravitacional. Além disso ela examina o papel das partículas de energía nula de Rindler e como elas se relacionam com o campo radiativo de partículas uniformemente aceleradas.

Palavras chave:
aceleração, radiação, efeito Unruh, energia nula de Rindler, bremsstrahlung.

Abstract

It has been known for some time that the classical concept of radiation is not covariant: for uniformly accelerated particles, it depends on the state of motion
of the observer relative to the particle emitting it. Moreover, recent literature in the field of Quantum Field Theory in Curved Spacetimes suggests a deep
connection between bremsstrahlung and the Unruh effect, where zero-Rindler energy particles have played a central role beyond constructing the radiation
contents of systems; as this is an unfamiliar concept, it seems unsettling to deal with particles having no energy. This thesis studies such a connection in
both the classical and quantum perspectives, showing how the quantum Unruh effect is responsible for the classical radiation detected for the electric and
gravitational cases, and studies the role of zero-Rindler-energy particles and how they relate to the radiative field of uniformly accelerated particles.

Keywords:
acceleration, radiation, Unruh effect, zero-Rindler-energy, bremsstrahlung.

– Chapter 1 –
Introduction

From the astronomer processing data obtained from their telescope, to the first year student directly observing their experiments in their laboratory classes,
radiation is the most widely used tool to probe the universe. This phenomenon is so present in nature that, as far as the knowledge of the author goes, most
animals have developed systems to perceive and interpret (a narrow band of the spectrum of) incoming electromagnetic radiation, i.e., eyes connected to a
central nervous system. One can easily make the point that having a clear and deep understanding of radiation, and how it is produced, should be a focal point
of a Physicist’s career.

Acceleration is one of the mechanisms responsible for the emission of radiation. This has intrigued the community since Larmor reported that the
radiated
electromagnetic power
by unit of solid angle
is proportional to the square of the acceleration of a charge
following an arbitrary trajectory,
a
purely mechanical quantity

[
1
]
. Even though this is now taught at almost every electrodynamics course as standard
knowledge,
1
1

1

Jackson
[
2
]
(arguably the most widely used textbook on the subject) dedicates an entire section to
the discussion of this result.
it has not been without controversy: Physicists of the caliber of Pauli
[
3
]
and
Feynman
[
4
]
raise arguments against the interpretation of Larmor’s formula for a charge undergoing uniform
acceleration. The former uses a solution of Maxwell’s equations originally found by Born
[
5
]
to (wrongly) argue that such a
field does not produce radiation, while the latter uses Einstein’s Equivalence Principle to argue no such radiation should be observed, and therefore, the class
of systems this formula can be applied to should be limited.

The classical aspects of this controversy were rigorously treated a couple of decades ago by Rohrlich and Fulton
[
6
]
,
Rohrlich
[
7
,
8
]
, and independently by
Boulware
[
9
]
. They showed that radiation
is not a covariant concept
, as the perception of radiative
phenomena is intrinsically associated to the observer’s state of motion: if an inertial observer detects radiation coming from a uniformly accelerated charge,
another observer in the coaccelerated frame of the charge
will not see it
. Moreover, an accelerated observer is causally disconnected from a part of
spacetime where it cannot receive signals from, and the apparent horizons delimiting these regions are responsible for the detectability of radiation.

As interesting as this classical answer is, it is in the realm of Quantum Field Theory in Curved Spacetimes (QFTCS) where this relationship can be understood
on the more fundamental levels. QFTCS is, currently, the best approach to study quantum phenomena in the presence of gravity. It is a semi-classical theory:
only the matter fields are quantized, and the gravitational interaction is treated in completely classical terms using Einstein’s General Theory of Relativity.
The standard Quantum Field Theory that is used to formulate widely accepted frameworks, like the Standard Model, disregards gravitational effects and therefore,
interesting (and sometimes counterintuitive) phenomena like Hawking radiation
[
10
]
are only apparent on the context of QFTCS.

The Unruh effect is one of such predictions (although it is a flat spacetime effect). It states that an observer undergoing uniform acceleration
a
will detect the inertial Minkowski vacuum as a
thermal bath of (Rindler) particles at the Unruh temperature
[
11
]

T_{\mathrm{U}}=\frac{\hbar a}{2\pi ck_{\mathrm{B}}}.

(1.1)

This is the unified interpretation of previous observations by Fulling and Davies, who respectively showed that the particle content of a quantum field theory
depends on the observer
[
12
]
, and that an accelerated observer registers radiation with a temperature proportional to
the acceleration
[
13
]
. This effect was also discovered independently in the context of
axiomatic quantum field
theory
, where Bisognano, Wichmann
[
14
,
15
]
and
Sewell
[
16
]
showed that the vacuum state is a thermal state with respect to the Lorentz boosts generators, therefore
generalizing the derivation of the Unruh effect to free and interacting fields
[
17
]
, i.e., meaning the thermal bath is composed of all
sorts of particles. The scale of this temperature is interesting as well: given the gravitational field near the surface of the earth of
9.81\ \mathrm{m/s^{2}}
, the corresponding Unruh temperature is
3.98\times 10^{-20}\ \mathrm{K}
; an amount which seems negligible, but large accelerations would create high
enough temperatures for the observer to be “burn to a crisp,” while inertial observers “freeze to death.”

Independently of how small this temperature might be, the Fulling-Davies-Unruh (FDU) thermal bath stimulates both the absorption and emission of Rindler
particles by uniformly accelerated charges, and it is responsible for the correspondence between the total number of particles seen by a commoving observer with
the number of particles measured by an inertial one
[
18
,
19
]
. These
particles will have zero energy from the accelerated observer perspective, which is due to the fact that only charges accelerated for an infinite amount of time
were considered. These findings are in agreement with previous literature reporting that the observation by an inertial observer of the emission of a Minkowski
particle by an accelerated detector corresponds with either the absorption or emission of a Rindler particle from the detector’s perspective
[
20
]
, meaning both
observers must agree if the fields change their state. The connection between the Unruh effect and the radiation of accelerated
charges has also been strengthened by other works such as Ref.
[
21
]
, where an experimental set up is proposed to show that
the quantum Unruh effect is codified in the purely classical Larmor radiation, thus proving the existence of the Unruh effect without the need to even carry out
the experiment, if one trusts classical electrodynamics. Recently, the role of zero-energy Rindler photons and the idea that Larmor radiation codifies the
Unruh effect has been further explored for scalar electrodynamics in Ref.
[
22
]
, showing that indeed only zero-energy
Rindler modes contribute to the description of the field in the radiation sector of spacetime.

As we have seen, the connection between acceleration and radiation has been thoroughly explored in the field of electrodynamics: the seminal work that showcases
this relationship is an electrodynamics paper, and the reconciliation between the observations of accelerated and inertial observers (in the classical realm)
was done using this well-understood interaction. However important electrodynamics is, it is not the only type of radiation used to survey the universe.
Gravitational radiation, first predicted by Einstein over a century
ago
[
23
,
24
]
, was proven to exist by observing binary mergers of black
holes in 2015
[
25
]
, giving us a new tool to test distinct predictions of gravitational
theories
[
26
]
.

Particularly, the idea of accelerated particles producing gravitational radiation is not an innovation; to mention a few examples:

\Citeauthor
bicakGravitationalRadiationUniformly1968
[
27
]
studied the radiative field (using Bondi’s
procedure
[
28
]
) of the solution originally proposed by

\Citeauthor
bonnorExactSolutionUniformly1964
[
29
]
for four accelerated particles in a Minkowski background;

\Citeauthor
hopperScatteringPointParticles2018
[
30
]
analyze the scattering of test particles and the resulting
gravitational waves around a Schwarzschild geometry; and Poisson
et al
produced a series of papers analyzing the radiational content of a system with
two components, one being much more massive than the
other
[
31
,
32
,
33
,
34
,
35
,
36
]
.
Other examples are the works by Bernar, Crispino, and Higuchi
[
37
]
, studying the quantum waves coming from a test particle
around a Schwarzschild black hole; these same authors study gravitational waves in de Sitter
spacetime
[
38
,
39
]
. More recently, Brito, Crispino and Higuchi showed than the
equivalence between the response rates of inertial and accelerated detectors interacting holds for the gravitational
case
[
40
]
, using modes found a couple of years before
[
41
]
.

In this thesis we aim to study what happens with a single uniformly accelerated particle, carrying the properties of electric charge and mass (separately), to

•

connect the observations of inertial observers in the asymptotic future with those of accelerated ones, both in the classical context and that of
QFTCS;

•

clarify the role of zero-Rindler-energy particles and modes, both in the classical and quantum contexts;

•

improve our understanding of the connection between breaking acceleration (bremsstrahlung) and the Unruh effect.

In order to achieve this, we will generalize the notion of Unruh modes defined for scalar electrodynamics to spin 1 and 2 fields, which provide us with
effective tools to relate the perspective of both observers, along with other tools of both mathematical physics and QFTCS to show how zero-Rindler-energy modes
are not simple mathematical objects and play a fundamental role in the description of radiative phenomena.

From this point forward we use natural units for the reduced Plank constant, the speed of light and Boltzmann’s constant, this is,
\hbar=c=k_{\mathrm{B}}=1
. We also need to set some conventions that are going to be used throughout this work.

1.1
Geometry elements and notation

The General Theory of Relativity
[
42
,
43
,
44
]
describes gravity as
an effect of the curvature of spacetime: in an almost symbiotic relationship the matter content of the universe evolves along the curved background, and the
curvature is influenced by the matter content of the universe.

In this theory, spacetime is modeled as a
d
-dimensional manifold
M
, a collection of points such that there exists a collection of subsets of
M
,
denoted by
2
2

2

Here
\mu
is an arbitrary index notation.

U_{\mu}
that are called open sets, and the union of these form the entire collection, i.e.,

M=\bigcup_{\mu}U_{\mu}.

(1.2)

For each of these open sets there exists at least one invertible mapping

x^{\mu}\!:\ U_{\mu}\longrightarrow V_{\mu}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}

(1.3)

known as
coordinate chart
that satisfies two important conditions:

Existance of diffeomorphisms:

between two opens
U_{\mu}
and
U_{\nu}
, with
\mu\neq\nu
, such that
U_{\mu}\cap U_{\nu}\neq\emptyset
, the mapping
x^{\mu}\circ(x^{\prime\nu})^{-1}\!:x^{\prime\nu}(U_{\mu}\cap U_{\nu})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow x^{\mu}(U_{\mu}\cap U_{\nu})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a smooth (
C^{\infty}
) bijection.

Haussdorf condition:

given two distinct points in the manifold
P,Q\in M
, there exist two disjoint open sets
U_{P}\subset M
and
U_{Q}\subset M
, such that
P\in U_{P}
and
Q\in U_{Q}
.

From here on forward to the end of the thesis we will work with 4-dimensional manifolds, representing one time parameter and 3 spatial directions. In general,
if we are not using the labels explicitly, we assign the time coordinate the 0-th index and 1, 2 and 3 to the spacelike ones.

We can define objects through their transformation properties in these manifolds. First, scalar functions are mappings defined from the manifold to the set of real numbers:
f\!:\ M\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
and they assign a value to each of the points of the manifold,
independently of the coordinate choice
. The set of all
scalar fields over
M
that are differentiable
k\geq 1
times is denoted by
C^{k}(M)
. From these fields we can define vector fields, which are to be
understood as linear combinations of derivations of these scalar fields: given a point
P\in U\subseteq M
, where the open
U
is covered by the
coordinate chart
x^{\mu}
, the vector field
\xi|_{P}\!:C^{k}(M)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is defined as

\xi|_{P}(f)=\sum_{\mu=0}^{3}\xi^{\mu}\left.\frac{\partial{f}}{\partial{x^{\mu}}}\right|_{P},

(1.4)

where
|_{P}
denotes evaluation at the point
P
. Here the coefficients
\xi^{\mu}
are known as
components and are dependent on the choice of coordinates: if we use a different coordinate set
x^{\prime\nu}
to cover
U
, the coefficients
\xi^{\prime\nu}
in
these new coordinates are related to
\xi^{\mu}
of the old ones via the linear relation (a.k.a. a transformation)

\xi^{\prime\nu}=\sum_{\mu=0}^{3}\frac{\partial{x^{\prime\nu}}}{\partial{x^{\mu}}}\xi^{\mu}.

(1.5)

The set of all vector fields defined in a point
P
are denoted
T_{P}(M)
, and this set has the structure of a vector space with
\{\partial/\partial x^{a}|_{P}\}
forming a basis.
3
3

3

From here on forward, if the coordinate chart is known, the basis will be denoted as
\partial_{a}
.
We can define also the dual
of vectors as applications
\omega\!:\ T_{P}(M)\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined as
\omega(\xi)=\omega_{a}\xi^{a}
. Again the coefficients
\omega_{a}

receive the name of components and transform under diffeomorphism as

\omega^{\prime}_{a}=\frac{\partial{x^{b}}}{\partial{x^{\prime a}}}\omega_{b}.

(1.6)

All covectors defined in a point
P
form a set denoted
T^{*}_{P}(M)
. Notice how we changed from the Greek indices labels to Latin ones, this is to
emphasize on the tensor properties of these objects as the book by Wald
[
44
]
does.

Tensors of the rank
\binom{p}{q}
, where
p
and
q
are nonnegative integers, are objects of the set
[T_{P}(M)]^{\otimes p}\otimes[T^{*}_{P}(M)]^{\otimes q}
, where
\otimes
denotes the
tensor product
, and for an object
\mathsf{T}
, its components transform as

\mathchoice{{T^{\prime}}^{{{a_{1}}{a_{2}}{\cdots}{a_{p}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.3529pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.67499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.37988pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.37988pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29091pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.69008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.84009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.10005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.84009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.10005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.67499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.30296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.30296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.85103pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.111pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b_{1}}{\cdots}{b_{q}}}}}{{T^{\prime}}^{{{a_{1}}{a_{2}}{\cdots}{a_{p}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.3529pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.67499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.37988pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.37988pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.29091pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.69008pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.84009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\ (1.7)

For example scalar fields are
\binom{0}{0}
tensors, vectors are
\binom{1}{0}
tensors, covectors are
\binom{0}{1}
tensors. From here on forward the
Latin indices of the beginning of the alphabet represent tensor components independent of the coordinate choice. Also, we will use the term tensor and its components
interchangeably.

In this thesis we follow the conventions of Ref.
[
45
]
. The first object we introduce for the description of the geometry of a
manifold is the metric. It describes the way we will measure distances inside the manifold. For an infinitesimal displacement
\mathrm{d}s
in a vicinity of the
point
P
that is mapped by coordinates
x^{a}
, the metric
g_{ab}
is defined as
4
4

4

This can be roughly thought of as a generalization Pitagoras’s theorem.

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=g_{ab}(P)\,\mathrm{d}x^{a}\mathrm{d}x^{b},

(1.8)

where the metric tensor is symmetric in its indices:
g_{ab}=g_{ba}
; and invertible. As
the point
P
is arbitrary,
g_{ab}
is defined in the entire manifold. The pair
(M,g_{ab})
is known as a
spacetime
. The metric has an inverse

g^{ab}
such that for every point in spacetime

g^{ac}g_{bc}=\delta^{a}_{b}=\begin{cases}1,&\text{if }a=b,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(1.9)

The symbol
\delta^{a}_{b}
is known as the Kroeneker delta. The other utility the metric (and its inverse) is to lower (raise) indices. For examples, for a
vector
\xi^{a}
and a covector
\omega_{a}
we have the associated covector and vector respectively:

\displaystyle\xi_{a}

\displaystyle=g_{ab}\xi^{b}

\displaystyle\omega^{a}

\displaystyle=g^{ab}\omega_{b}.

(1.10)

The same can be applied to more general objects but this suffices to showcase this property. This has an important application, as we can classify (co)vectors
using the value of the product between a vector and its associated covector. For a vector
\xi^{a}
we say that it is timelike if
\xi^{a}\xi_{a}<0
, we call
it lightlike if
\xi^{a}\xi_{a}=0
, and spacelike if
\xi^{a}\xi_{a}>0
.

From the metric we can define the Christoffel symbols of the second kind

(1.11)

(1.12)

If all components of this tensor are zero the spacetime is flat. The connection is also useful to define a derivative, whose components transform as a tensor,
unlike the partial derivative. This new object is known as a
covariant derivative
and its components are given, if acting over an arbitrary rank
\binom{p}{q}
tensor by

}}{\makebox[3.75208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.67499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.84009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.10005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.84009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.10005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.67499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.30296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.30296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.85103pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.111pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b_{1}}{b_{2}}{\cdots}{d}}}}{T^{{{a_{1}}{a_{2}}{\cdots}{a_{p}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.3529pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.3529pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.67499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.84009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.10005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.84009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.10005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.67499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.30296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.30296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.85103pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.111pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b_{1}}{b_{2}}{\cdots}{d}}}}{T^{{{a_{1}}{a_{2}}{\cdots}{a_{p}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.3529pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.3529pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.67499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.84009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.10005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.84009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.10005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.67499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.30296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.30296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.85103pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.111pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b_{1}}{b_{2}}{\cdots}{d}}}}{T^{{{a_{1}}{a_{2}}{\cdots}{a_{p}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.3529pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.46666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.3529pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.75208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.67499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.84009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.10005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.28764pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.84009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.10005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.25pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.67499pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.625pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.30296pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.30296pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.85103pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.111pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b_{1}}{b_{2}}{\cdots}{d}}}}.

(1.13)

This definition is such that the covariant derivative is torsionless and metric compatible, meaning the properties

\displaystyle\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}f

\displaystyle=\nabla_{b}\nabla_{a}f,\ \forall f\in C^{k}(M),

\displaystyle\nabla_{c}g_{ab}=0,

(1.14)

are satisfied.

The covariant derivative is important to define the concept of Killing vector: a vector field
\xi^{a}
that solves the equation

\nabla_{a}\xi_{b}+\nabla_{b}\xi_{a}=0.

(1.15)

This is equivalent to the Lie derivative
[
46
]

of the metric tensor in this direction

\mathscr{L}_{\xi}g_{ab}=0.

(1.16)

Lie derivatives measure the flow of a field along the direction of a vector, a generalization of the notion of directional derivative of vector calculus that
does not depend on the vector-space properties of the underlying structure. In this case this implies the metric is kept invariant along the direction of the
Killing field, and therefore characterizes the symmetries of the spacetime. This notion is central to the definition of gravitational conserved
charges
[
47
,
48
]
.

A spacetime is called globally hyperbolic if there is a Cauchy surface, which is a 3-dimensional slice of the spacetime which is crossed by every inextensible
causal curve only once. Cauchy surfaces and globally hyperbolic spacetimes play a fundamental role in the description of quantum phenomena in curved
spacetimes: the evolution of the fields can be predicted using data from this surface
[
49
,
50
]
.
In particular, the existence of a timelike Killing field implies the spacetime is globally hyperbolic by allowing us to define a hypersurface ortogonal to these
vectors that satisfies this condition.

Other interesting geometric objects defined from contractions of the Riemann tensor are the Ricci curvature and Ricci scalar

\displaystyle R_{ab}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{R^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{c}{b}}}}{R^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{c}{b}}}}{R^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{c}{b}}}}{R^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{c}{b}}}},

\displaystyle R=\mathchoice{R^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{R^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{R^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{R^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{R^{{{b}{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}{a}}}}{R^{{{b}{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}{a}}}}{R^{{{b}{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}{a}}}}{R^{{{b}{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}{a}}}},

(1.17)

respectively. These objects are then used to write Einstein’s field equation

R_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}g_{ab}R=8\pi G\,T_{ab},

(1.18)

that showcases the interaction between gravity (the geometry in the left hand side) and matter (the energy-momentum tensor of the right hand side); the coupling
constant
G
is known as Newton’s gravitational constant and in SI units its given by
(6.6743\pm 0.0002)\times 10^{-11}\ \mathrm{m^{3}kg^{-1}s^{-2}}
.

1.2
Minkowski and Rindler spacetimes: the description of uniformly accelerated particles in special relativity

Four-dimensional Minkowski spacetime is first introduced when discussing special relativity as a generalization of 3-dimensional Euclidean space. It
corresponds with a vacuum solution of Einstein’s field equations (
1.18
) that is flat everywhere. Mathematically, we describe it as
the
\mathbb{R}^{4}
manifold endowed with the Minkowski metric
\eta_{ab}=\diag(-1,1,1,1)
and coordinates
(t,x,y,z)
, where the negative element of
the metric corresponds with the time coordinate
t
and the positive ones to the space components
(x,y,z)
. One can define a timelike and future oriented
Killing vector
(\partial_{t})^{a}=(1,0,0,0)
which is perpendicular to the constant time Cauchy hypersurface defined by
\{t_{0}\}\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}
for
any
constant
t_{0}
.

Figure 1.1:
The divisions of Minkowski spacetime.

The dashed lines represent the coordinate axis of the system
(t,z)
and the solid lines represent the lightlike surfaces that divide the spacetime in
four regions: the Left and Right Rindler Wedges (LRW and RRW respectively), and the Expanding and Contracting Degenerate Kasner Universes (EDKU and CDKU
respectively). The conditions satisfied by the coordinates on each one are also shown.

Choosing an arbitrary space direction (in this thesis we pick
z
), we can divide Minkowski spacetime in 4 disjoint regions:
the left and right Rindler
wedges
(LRW and RRW), and the
expanding (or contracting) degenerate Kasner universes
(EDKU and CDKU). See Fig.
1.1
for their definitions.
Each of these regions is a spacetime on its own right, and they are covered by their own coordinated charts called
Rindler coordinates
(sometimes in
older literature they are called
radar coordinates
). Given a strictly positive parameter
a
, these are

RRW:

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=a^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{a\xi}\sinh(a\lambda),

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=a^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{a\xi}\cosh(a\lambda);

(1.19a)

EDKU:

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=a^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{a\eta}\cosh(a\zeta),

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=a^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{a\eta}\sinh(a\zeta);

(1.19b)

LRW:

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=a^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{a\bar{\xi}}\sinh(a\bar{\lambda}),

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=-a^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{a\bar{\xi}}\cosh(a\bar{\lambda});

(1.19c)

CDKU:

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=-a^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-a\bar{\eta}}\cosh(a\bar{\zeta}),

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=a^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-a\bar{\eta}}\sinh(a\bar{\zeta});

(1.19d)

where all parameters run from
-\infty
to
\infty
and
(x,y)
remain unaltered. These can be visualized
in Fig.
1.2
. The line elements in these patches, using
these coordinates, are given by

RRW:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}s^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{2a\xi}(-\mathrm{d}\lambda^{2}+\mathrm{d}{\xi}^{2})+\mathrm{d}x^{2}+\mathrm{d}y^{2},

(1.20a)

EDKU:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}s^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{2a\eta}(-\mathrm{d}{\eta}^{2}+\mathrm{d}{\zeta}^{2})+\mathrm{d}x^{2}+\mathrm{d}y^{2},

(1.20b)

LRW:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}s^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{2a\bar{\xi}}(-\mathrm{d}\bar{\lambda}^{2}+\mathrm{d}{\bar{\xi}}^{2})+\mathrm{d}x^{2}+\mathrm{d}y^{2},

(1.20c)

CDKU:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}s^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{2a\bar{\eta}}(-\mathrm{d}{\bar{\eta}}^{2}+\mathrm{d}{\bar{\zeta}}^{2})+\mathrm{d}x^{2}+\mathrm{d}y^{2}.

(1.20d)

Even thought these divisions are also flat spacetimes, the coordinate choice we’ve made to describe each of these does not yield null connection coefficients; in the RRW
we find the nonzero components to be

(1.22)

From the line elements we can also see that as the metric is independent of the time coordinate in both the LRW and RRW, the timelike vector field induced by
this coordinate will be a Killing vector
[
43
,
44
]
, and thus, we have (infinitely many) Cauchy surfaces in these
patches, therefore quantization can be carried out in them. This is not the case for either the EDKU or CDKU.

Figure 1.2:
Foliation of the regions of Minkowski spacetime.

All coordinates
\lambda
,
\xi
,
\eta
,
\zeta
,
\bar{\lambda}
,
\bar{\xi}
,
\bar{\eta}
, and
\bar{\zeta}
run from
-\infty
to
\infty
, and cover the entire patch where they are defined. Arrows indicate the directions where coordinates grow in value. We also showcase the
patches where Eqs. (
1.19
) hold.

Let us consider a particle in rectilinear motion along the the
z
direction with proper time
\tau
. We can find the 4-velocity
u^{b}
and 4-acceleration

a^{b}
of this motion; if these quantities satisfy

\frac{\mathrm{d}a^{b}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}+(a_{c}a^{c})u^{b}=0,

(1.23)

we say the particle is undergoing
uniform
acceleration

[
8
]
. The trajectory

\displaystyle t(\tau)

\displaystyle=a^{-1}\sinh(a\tau),

\displaystyle x(\tau)

\displaystyle=y(\tau)=0,

\displaystyle z(\tau)

\displaystyle=a^{-1}\cosh(a\tau),

\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R},

(1.24)

satisfies condition (
1.23
). If we compute the 4-acceleration of this trajectory, we find that
a^{b}a_{b}=a^{2}
, a constant
that is interpreted as the modulus of the acceleration of the particle and receives the name of
acceleration parameter
.
We can also see that the motion of this particle is clearly restricted to the RRW, and in Rindler coordinates its motion can be more simply described, since
this worldline is parameterized as

\displaystyle\lambda(\tau)

\displaystyle=\tau,

\displaystyle\xi(\tau)=x(\tau)

\displaystyle=y(\tau)=0.

(1.25)

This means Rindler coordinates in the RRW (
1.19a
) are associated with the worldline followed by uniformly accelerating observers with a modulus of
acceleration
a
. The observer following the trajectory (
1.25
) is called a fiduciary observer.

1.3
The scalar field

Let us begin by describing the process of quantization of a massless scalar field
\phi
. The dynamics of this field are described by the Klein-Gordon Lagrangian
density

\mathcal{L}_{\text{scalar}}=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{-g}\,\nabla_{a}\phi\nabla^{a}\phi,

(1.26)

where
\nabla_{a}
corresponds to the metric compatible
5
5

5

This means
\nabla_{a}g_{bc}=0
.
covariant derivative, and
g
is the determinant of the metric. The corresponding Euler-Lagrange
equation for
\phi
is given by

\nabla^{a}\nabla_{a}\phi=0.

(1.27)

If we have two solutions of this equation, namely
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
, the Klein-Gordon inner product is defined as

\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{\text{s}}=\mathrm{i}\iiint_{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Sigma^{a}\,\sqrt{|h|},\left[\overline{f_{1}(x)}\nabla_{a}f_{2}(x)-f_{2}(x)\nabla_{a}\overline{f_{1}(x)}\right],

(1.28)

where
\Sigma
is a Cauchy surface with future oriented element
\mathrm{d}\Sigma^{a}
,
h
is the determinant of the induced metric in the Cauchy hypersurface and
the overline represents complex conjugation. This definition is such that it remains independent of the choice of
\Sigma
, i.e., it is
conserved
. It
satisfies the properties
\overline{\langle f_{1},f_{2}\rangle_{\text{s}}}=\langle f_{2},f_{1}\rangle_{\text{s}}=-\langle\overline{f_{1}},\overline{f_{2}}\rangle_{\text{s}}
. All versions of the inner product must satisfy these properties.

For Minkowski spacetime, using inertial Cartesian coordinates
(t,x,y,z)
, Eq. (
1.27
) reads

-\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial t^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial x^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial y^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial z^{2}}=0,

(1.29)

from where we see that a simple solution is given by positive-energy plane waves:

g_{\mathbf{k}}(t,\mathbf{x})={(16\pi^{3}k)}^{-1/2}\exp[\mathrm{i}(\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}-kt)],

(1.30)

where
\mathbf{k}=(k_{x},k_{y},k_{z})\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
is the wave vector that characterizes the mode,
k=|\mathbf{k}|
, and the coefficient has been chosen in
such way that these are orthonormalized under the Klein-Gordon inner product, i.e.,

\displaystyle\langle g_{\mathbf{k}},g_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\rangle_{\text{s}}=\mathrm{i}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{x}\left(\overline{g_{\mathbf{k}}}\frac{\partial g_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}{\partial t}-g_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}\frac{\partial\overline{g_{\mathbf{k}}}}{\partial t}\right)=\delta^{3}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}),

(1.31a)

\displaystyle\langle g_{\mathbf{k}},\overline{g_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\rangle_{\text{s}}=\mathrm{i}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{x}\left(\overline{g_{\mathbf{k}}}\frac{\partial\overline{g_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}}{\partial t}-\overline{g_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\frac{\partial\overline{g_{\mathbf{k}}}}{\partial t}\right)=0,

(1.31b)

which means that the positive energy plane waves form a complete set of solutions of Eq. (
1.27
). In the classical realm we can expand any solution of
Eq. (
1.27
) as

\phi(x)=\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}\left[\phi_{\mathbf{k}}g_{\mathbf{k}}(x)+\tilde{\phi}_{\mathbf{k}}\overline{g_{\mathbf{k}}(x)}\right],

(1.32)

where
\phi_{\mathbf{k}}=\langle g_{\mathbf{k}},\phi\rangle_{\text{s}}
and

\tilde{\phi}_{\mathbf{k}}=\langle\phi,\overline{g_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}}}\rangle_{\text{s}}
.
Now the field can be quantized by imposing the
equal time commutation relations

\displaystyle[\hat{\phi}(t,\mathbf{x}),\hat{\phi}(t,\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle[\hat{\phi}(t,\mathbf{x}),\hat{\Pi}(t,\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\delta^{3}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}),

\displaystyle[\hat{\Pi}(t,\mathbf{x}),\hat{\Pi}(t,\mathbf{x}^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=0,

(1.33)

where the canonical momentum is defined using the time derivative

\Pi\coloneqq\frac{\partial{\mathcal{L}}}{\partial{(\nabla^{t}\phi)}}=\frac{\partial{\phi}}{\partial{t}}.

(1.34)

The field is expanded as

\hat{\phi}(x)=\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{k}\left[\hat{a}(\overline{g_{\mathbf{k}}})g_{\mathbf{k}}(x)+\hat{a}^{\dagger}(g_{\mathbf{k}})\overline{g_{\mathbf{k}}(x)}\right],

(1.35)

where
\hat{a}(\overline{g_{\mathbf{k}}})\coloneqq\langle g_{\mathbf{k}},\hat{\phi}\rangle_{\text{s}}
are the annihilation operators associated to the positive energy mode
g_{\mathbf{k}}
and
\hat{a}^{\dagger}(g_{\mathbf{k}})\coloneqq\langle\hat{\phi},\overline{g_{\mathbf{k}}}\rangle_{\text{s}}
is the corresponding creation operator. As such, there
is a state of the field
|0_{\mathrm{M}}\rangle
known as the Minkowski vacuum which defined as
\hat{a}(\overline{g_{\mathbf{k}}})|0_{\mathrm{M}}\rangle=0

for all
\mathbf{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The excited states correspond to the Fock space spawned as successive applications of
\hat{a}^{\dagger}(g_{\mathbf{k}})
over the
Minkowski vacuum.

We can now study the Klein-Gordon equation restricted to the RRW,
6
6

6

Chosen arbitrarily: we can pick either of the Rindler wedges.
the field equation (
1.27
) in Rindler coordinates is
given by

\mathrm{e}^{-2a\xi}\left(-\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial\lambda^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial\xi^{2}}\right)+\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial x^{2}}+\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial y^{2}}=0,

(1.36)

which is not as simple as Eq. (
1.29
). However, solutions can be written using right Rindler modes

v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{\sinh(\pi\omega/a)}{4\pi^{4}a}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{\perp}-\omega\lambda)}\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{i}\omega/a}(a^{-1}k_{\perp}\mathrm{e}^{a\xi}),

(1.37)

where
\mathrm{K}_{\nu}(z)
is the modified Bessel function of the second kind and order
\nu
, and the numbers
\mathbf{k}_{\perp}=(k_{x},k_{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}
along with
\omega\geq 0
are used to identify the corresponding mode: they represent the transverse momentum and Rindler energy
respectively. Again, the coefficient is chosen in such a way that right Rindler modes are orthonormalized. This time we consider the integration over the
Cauchy surface
\Sigma^{\mathrm{R}}=\{(\lambda_{0},\xi,\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\ |\ \lambda_{0}=\text{constant}\}\cong\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which is
generated by the vector
(\partial_{\lambda})^{a}
, and we find

\displaystyle\langle v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}}\rangle_{\text{s}}=\mathrm{i}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}\xi\ \mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\left(\overline{v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}}\frac{\partial{v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}}}}{\partial{\lambda}}-v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}}\frac{\partial{\overline{v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}}}}{\partial{\lambda}}\right)=\delta(\omega-\omega^{\prime})\,\delta^{2}(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}),

(1.38a)

\displaystyle\langle v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},\overline{v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}}}\rangle_{\text{s}}=\mathrm{i}\iiint_{\mathbb{R}^{3}}\mathrm{d}\xi\ \mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{x}_{\perp}\left(\overline{v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}}\frac{\partial{\overline{v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}}}}}{\partial{\lambda}}-\overline{v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}}}\frac{\partial{\overline{v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}}}}{\partial{\lambda}}\right)=0.

(1.38b)

The quantized field in the RRW is given by

\hat{\phi}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\left[\hat{a}(\overline{v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}})\,v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}(x)+\hat{a}^{\dagger}(v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}})\,\overline{v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}(x)}\right],

(1.39)

after imposing the equal time commutation relations

[\hat{\phi}(\lambda,\xi,\mathbf{x}_{\perp}),\hat{\Pi}(\lambda,\xi^{\prime},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\perp})]=\mathrm{i}\delta(\xi-\xi^{\prime})\,\delta^{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp}-\mathbf{x}^{\prime}_{\perp}),

(1.40)

with
\hat{\Pi}(\lambda,\xi,\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\coloneqq\partial\hat{\phi}(\lambda,\xi,\mathbf{x}_{\perp})/\partial\lambda
, and all other commutation relations
being zero. We can define the right Rindler vacuum
|0_{\mathrm{R}}\rangle
by
\hat{a}(\overline{v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}})|0_{\mathrm{R}}\rangle=0
,
and the corresponding Fock space is generated by successive applications of
\hat{a}^{\dagger}(v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}})
over the Rindler vacuum,
thus

|n_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\mathrm{R}}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n!}}\left[\hat{a}^{\dagger}(v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}})\right]^{n}|0_{\mathrm{R}}\rangle,

(1.41)

is interpreted as the
n
-th excited state for a right mode with numbers
\omega
and
\mathbf{k}_{\perp}
.

For the LRW the field equation has the same form as Eq. (
1.36
)

\mathrm{e}^{-2a\bar{\xi}}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial\bar{\lambda}^{2}}-\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial\bar{\xi}^{2}}\right)-\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial x^{2}}-\frac{\partial^{2}\phi}{\partial y^{2}}=0,

(1.42)

and the left Rindler Modes are given by

v^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{\sinh(\pi\omega/a)}{4\pi^{4}a}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{\perp}+\omega\bar{\lambda})}\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{i}\omega/a}(a^{-1}k_{\perp}\mathrm{e}^{a\bar{\xi}}).

(1.43)

Quantization proceeds in the same fashion as for the RRW and the associated Rindler vacuum is kept the same, but we have to consider the future oriented vector
field
(-\partial_{\bar{\lambda}})^{a}
as the orientation when computing the inner product.

Both the left and right Rindler modes can be extended to cover the entirety of Minkowski spacetime by means of analytic
continuation
[
51
]
. This is done using the Bogoliubov transformations
[
49
,
50
]

v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{\perp}}}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}k_{z}}{\sqrt{4\pi k_{0}}}\left(\alpha^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega k_{z}k_{\perp}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(-k_{0}t+k_{z}z)}+\beta^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega k_{z}k_{\perp}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(-k_{0}t+k_{z}z)}\right),

(1.44)

v^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{\perp}}}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}k_{z}}{\sqrt{4\pi k_{0}}}\left(\alpha^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega k_{z}k_{\perp}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(-k_{0}t+k_{z}z)}+\beta^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega k_{z}k_{\perp}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}(-k_{0}t+k_{z}z)}\right).

(1.45)

The
coefficients used in this expression are

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega k_{z}k_{\perp}}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\pi\omega/(2a)}}{\sqrt{4\pi k_{0}a\sinh(\pi\omega/a)}}\left(\frac{k_{0}+k_{z}}{k_{0}-k_{z}}\right)^{-\mathrm{i}\omega/(2a)},

(1.46a)

\displaystyle\beta^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega k_{z}k_{\perp}}=-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\omega/(2a)}}{\sqrt{4\pi k_{0}a\sinh(\pi\omega/a)}}\left(\frac{k_{0}+k_{z}}{k_{0}-k_{z}}\right)^{-\mathrm{i}\omega/(2a)},

(1.46b)

\displaystyle\alpha^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega k_{z}k_{\perp}}=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\pi\omega/(2a)}}{\sqrt{4\pi k_{0}a\sinh(\pi\omega/a)}}\left(\frac{k_{0}-k_{z}}{k_{0}+k_{z}}\right)^{-\mathrm{i}\omega/(2a)},

(1.46c)

\displaystyle\beta^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega k_{z}k_{\perp}}=-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\omega/(2a)}}{\sqrt{4\pi k_{0}a\sinh(\pi\omega/a)}}\left(\frac{k_{0}-k_{z}}{k_{0}+k_{z}}\right)^{-\mathrm{i}\omega/(2a)}.

(1.46d)

From here we can note that these analytically extended modes can be recombined to get rid of any inertial negative energy modes with regards to inertial time
t
, into what is known as Unruh modes:

\displaystyle w_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{1}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\omega/a}\overline{v^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega\,-\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}}}{\sqrt{1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\omega/a}}},

\displaystyle w_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{2}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{v^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\omega/a}\overline{v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\,-\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}}}{\sqrt{1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\omega/a}}},

(1.47)

a set of solutions of the Klein-Gordon equation satisfying the normalization conditions

\displaystyle\langle w_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma},w_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}\rangle_{\mathrm{s}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}\,\delta(\omega-\omega^{\prime})\,\delta^{2}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}),

\displaystyle\langle w_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma},\overline{w_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}}^{\sigma^{\prime}}}\rangle_{\mathrm{s}}

\displaystyle=0.

(1.48)

Moreover, we can use these to quantize the field

\hat{\phi}(x)=\sum_{\sigma=1}^{2}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\left[\hat{a}(\overline{w_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma}})\,w_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma}\!(x)+\hat{a}^{\dagger}(w_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma})\,\overline{w_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma}\!(x)}\right],

(1.49)

and as these modes are positive energy regarding Minkowski time, the corresponding Unruh annihilation operator acts on the Minkowski vacuum as
\hat{a}(\overline{w_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma}})|0_{\mathrm{M}}\rangle=0
for all
\sigma
,
\omega
and
\mathbf{k}_{\perp}
. Independently of this, the
set of numbers characterizing these modes are related to the quantities as seen by an accelerated observer, specially the Rindler energy
\omega
; thus they
are a useful tool to connect the physics of inertial and accelerated observers.

In inertial coordinates, scalar Unruh modes can be written explicitly as a linear combination of positive energy Minkowski modes by means of an integral over a
free parameter known as the rapidity
[
51
]
:

w_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma}\!(t,\mathbf{x}_{\perp},z)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{\perp}}}{\sqrt{2a}(2\pi)^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}{\vartheta}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(-1)^{\sigma}\vartheta\omega/a}\exp\left[\mathrm{i}k_{\perp}(z\sinh\vartheta-t\cosh\vartheta)\right],

(1.50)

where
\sigma=1,2
. This representation has different forms depending on the location of the pair
(t,z)
due to the conditional convergence of the
integral of the right hand side. Following this idea, Eq. (
1.50
) can be understood as the definition of scalar Unruh modes in inertial coordinates as
distributions. Explicit forms of the scalar Unruh modes in the different patches of Minkowski spacetime are to be introduced as necessary.

Using the Bogoliubov transformations relating left and right Rindler modes with the inertial plane waves, we can show the Minkowski vacuum is proportional to an entangled
state of left and right excitations:

|0_{\mathrm{M}}\rangle\propto\prod_{\omega,\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}\left(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi n\omega/a}|n_{\omega\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{L}}\rangle\otimes|n_{\omega\mathbf{k}}^{\mathrm{R}}\rangle\right).

(1.51)

The Unruh effect is found when taking the reduced density matrix by tracing out the left Rindler modes,
\hat{\rho}_{\mathrm{R}}=\mathrm{tr}_{\mathrm{L}}\,|0_{\mathrm{M}}\rangle\langle 0_{\mathrm{M}}|
, and identifying this operator corresponds with a thermal density matrix for each of the
positive energies, each having a partition function
Z=(1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\omega/a})^{-1}
, exactly the partition function of the grand-canonical
ensemble with coldness
\beta=2\pi/a
, which coincides with the Unruh temperature from Eq. (
1.1
), i.e.,
T_{\mathrm{U}}\equiv\beta^{-1}
.

A thorough revision of the concepts discussed in this chapter can be found in the review by

[
52
]

[
52
]
. Further insight can be gained from
\Citeauthor
higuchiEntanglementVacuumLeft2017
[
51
]
, an excellent
work that contains the most detailed exposition on scalar the modes in literature.

– Chapter 2 –
Electromagnetic Radiation

This chapter is based on the results reported in the first paper published from this doctoral project
[
53
]
.
We deal with a classical charge that is accelerated and study the field arising from it using vector Unruh modes for both the classical and quantum
descriptions.

2.1
The electromagnetic interaction

Electrodynamics is one of the most well-established theories in all of physics. Thorough and modern treatises have been written about the classical aspects of
the subject (e.g. Refs
[
2
,
54
,
55
]
), and its
foundations have been a collaborative effort spanning several centuries which were consolidated by Maxwell in the 1860s. Quantum electrodynamics
is
1
1

1

This is even claimed by science outreach articles like Ref.
[
56
]
.
“the best-tested theory in physics,” and also
has a rich history: it started its development hand in hand with quantum mechanics, giving its own predictions without counterpart in the classical description,
such as Compton scattering
[
57
]
and pair production
[
58
]
. Here we present elements of
electrodynamics in covariant fashion, i.e., independently of the background geometry.

Given an arbitrary spacetime, the electromagnetic field is described by the
Faraday skew-symmetric tensor
F_{ab}
, which encodes the electric field
\mathbf{E}
and the magnetic flux density
\mathbf{B}
: an observer with 4-velocity
u^{a}
will report these fields to be

\displaystyle E_{a}

\displaystyle=F_{ab}u^{b},

and

\displaystyle B_{d}=\frac{1}{2}\epsilon_{abcd}u^{a}F^{bc},

(2.1)

where
\epsilon_{abcd}
is the Levi-Civita tensor, a covariantly constant totally anti-symmetric tensor satisfying
\epsilon_{0123}=\sqrt{-g}
in
all coordinate systems. Given the charge and current distribution
j^{a}=(\rho,\mathbf{J})
, the dynamics of these fields are governed by Maxwell’s equations
2
2

2

Using
Heaviside-Lorentz units
[
59
]
.

\displaystyle\nabla_{a}F^{ab}=-j^{b},

(2.2a)

\displaystyle\nabla_{a}F_{bc}+\nabla_{b}F_{ca}+\nabla_{c}F_{ab}=0.

(2.2b)

The Faraday 2-form can also be obtained as the derivation of the 4-potential

F_{ab}=\nabla_{a}A_{b}-\nabla_{b}A_{a},

(2.3)

and therefore, given a smooth scalar field
\Lambda
, the transformation

A_{a}\quad\longrightarrow\quad\widetilde{A}_{a}=A_{a}-\nabla_{a}\Lambda,

(2.4)

is known as a Gauge transformation that keeps the Faraday tensor invariant.

The field equations (
2.2a
) can be obtained as the Euler-Lagrange equations from a well-known action principle, namely

I_{\text{EM}}=\iiiint_{M}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\mathcal{L}_{\text{EM}}=-\iiiint_{M}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\sqrt{-g}\left(\frac{1}{4}F^{ab}F_{ab}-j_{a}A^{a}\right),

(2.5)

by variation of the 4-potential:
\delta A_{a}
, while Eq. (
2.2b
) follows from the skew-symmetry properties of
F_{ab}
. The Lagrangian
density defined in Eq. (
2.5
) is independent of the time derivative of
A_{0}
, meaning this component of the 4-potential
behaves, using the language of Classical Mechanics, as a cyclic coordinate. A consequence of this is that if we were to try to quantize the electromagnetic
field, we cannot impose commutation relations using this variable, because it has no associated generalized 4-momentum.

This inconvenience can be circumvented by adding a

gauge fixing term
to the action

I_{\text{EM}}^{\text{gf}}=-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\iiiint_{M}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,(\nabla_{a}A^{a})^{2},

(2.6)

where
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
is a parameter. If we consider the total action

I_{\text{EM}}^{\text{tot}}\coloneqq I_{\text{EM}}+I_{\text{EM}}^{\text{gf}},

(2.7)

the new field equation is given by

\nabla_{a}F^{ab}-\alpha^{-1}\nabla^{b}\nabla_{a}A^{a}=-j^{b}.

(2.8)

Comparing Eq. (
2.2a
) with (
2.8
), we can see they differ by a single term, which can be disregarded if the 4-potential satisfies the Lorenz condition

\nabla_{a}A^{a}=0,

(2.9)

which implies that the field equation reduces to

\nabla_{b}\nabla^{b}A_{a}=j_{a},

(2.10)

a wave equation for the vector-valued 4-potential. Another way of recovering this form is using the so called Feynman-gauge, this is, by fixing
\alpha=1
.

If we consider two solutions
\{A^{(1)},A^{(2)}\}
of the homogeneous electromagnetic field equation

\nabla_{b}\nabla^{b}A_{a}=0,

(2.11)

we can compute the generalized Klein-Gordon inner product
[
60
]
between them

\langle A^{(1)},A^{(2)}\rangle_{\text{v}}\coloneqq\mathrm{i}\iiint_{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Sigma_{a}\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\left(\overline{A_{b}^{(1)}}\Pi_{(2)}^{ab}-A_{b}^{(2)}\overline{\Pi_{(1)}^{ab}}\right),

(2.12)

where we have used the generalized momenta of the electromagnetic potential, defined as usual
\Pi^{ab}\coloneqq{\partial\mathcal{L}^{\text{tot}}_{\text{EM}}}/{\partial(\partial_{a}A_{b})}
. These are computed explicitly in the Feynman gauge:

\Pi^{ab}=\sqrt{-g}\,\left(\nabla^{b}A^{a}-\nabla^{a}A^{b}-g^{ab}\nabla_{c}A^{c}\right).

(2.13)

Having briefly reviewed the aspects of the electromagnetic interaction that are fundamental for our study, we proceed to introduce the physical set-up of moving
charge we are interested in describing.

2.2
The uniformly accelerated electric charge

As we have established, the trajectory described in Eqs. (
1.24
) and (
1.25
)
has uniform acceleration. We are interested in describing a point charge
q
moving along this path. The corresponding 4-current describing the charge can be
written in inertial and right Rindler coordinates

j_{\infty}^{b}(x)=aq\,\delta^{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\,\delta(z-\sqrt{a^{-2}+t^{2}})\,[z(\partial_{t})^{b}+t(\partial_{z})^{b}]=q\,\delta(\xi)\,\delta^{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\,(\partial_{\lambda})^{b},

(2.14)

respectively. Here
\delta(x)
is the Dirac delta distribution
[
61
,
62
]
, and we
used the
\infty
symbol to denote this charge is moving for an infinite amount of its proper time. However, we would like to deal with
compactly
supported
3
3

3

Given a function
f\!:M\to\mathbb{C}
, we call the support of
f
to the subset of the domain where the function takes nonzero
values, i.e.,
\supp f\coloneqq\{x\in M\,|\,f(x)\neq 0\}
.
currents (for reasons that will be made clear in Sect.
2.4
), which is
not the case for the one described in Eq. (
2.14
): the support is not bounded.
4
4

4

We mean this in the mathematical sense: there is
no finite radius for which a 3-sphere contains all the events in the support.

There are many sensible ways to find a suitable current for our purposes. The route we took during the development of this research consists of taking a charge
following an inertial trajectory, to then accelerate it for a finite amount of proper time
2T
, and then return it back to (a different) inertial trajectory;
after which we introduce an arbitrary cutoff when the charge is turned on and off, to ensure the support of it is compact and the 4-velocity is continuous. When
all quantities of interest are computed, we will return to the physical situation described above using a limiting process. A schematic picture of this
situation is presented on Fig.
2.1
.

Figure 2.1:
Movement of the charge.

The purple and green portions correspond to the inertial trajectories and the red one to the accelerated part. We also show the time intervals where the
charge is accelerated. The Cauchy surfaces are in blue and labeled with
\Sigma_{\pm}
. The solid portion of the trajectory corresponds to the compactified
current, while the dotted part corresponds to the limit
L\to\infty
.

The worldline followed by an inertial charge, which is suddenly accelerated with constant 4-acceleration
a
for a finite period of proper time
2T
to then
return to an inertial trajectory, can be written as a function of inertial time

\chi^{a}(t)=\begin{cases}\bm{(}t,0,0,a^{-1}\cosh(aT)-\tanh(aT)[t+a^{-1}\sinh(aT)]\bm{)},&\text{if $t\leq-a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$},\\
\bm{(}t,0,0,(a^{-2}+t^{2})^{1/2}\bm{)},&\text{if $|t|<a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$},\\
\bm{(}t,0,0,a^{-1}\cosh(aT)+\tanh(aT)[t-a^{-1}\sinh(aT)]\bm{)},&\text{if $t\geq a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$},\end{cases}

(2.15)

which is smooth both in the position and velocity at
t=\pm a^{-1}\sinh(aT)
, but this is not the case for the acceleration. This can be remedied by
considering that the acceleration from 0 to
a
is smooth over a time interval much shorter than
T
, which we model here as a sudden spike in acceleration.

Using this as the trajectory of the charge, we can write the nonzero components of the 4-current
in inertial coordinates
as

\tilde{j}^{t}_{\infty}(x)=\begin{cases}q\cosh(aT)\,\delta^{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\,\Delta_{+}(z,t),&\text{if $t\leq-a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$},\\
aqz\,\delta^{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\,\delta(z-\sqrt{a^{-2}+t^{2}}),&\text{if $|t|<a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$},\\
q\cosh(aT)\,\delta^{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\,\Delta_{-}(z,t),&\text{if $t\geq a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$},\end{cases}

(2.16a)

\tilde{j}^{z}_{\infty}(x)=\begin{cases}q\sinh(aT)\,\delta^{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\,\Delta_{+}(z,t),&\text{if $t\leq-a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$},\\
aqt\,\delta^{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\,\delta(z-\sqrt{a^{-2}+t^{2}}),&\text{if $|t|<a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$},\\
q\sinh(aT)\,\delta^{2}(\mathbf{x}_{\perp})\,\Delta_{-}(z,t),&\text{if $t\geq a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$},\end{cases}

(2.16b)

with all other components being zero and

\Delta_{\pm}(z,t)\coloneqq\delta\bm{(}z-a^{-1}\sech(aT)\pm t\tanh(aT)\bm{)}.

(2.17)

We can note that in the interval defined by
|t|<a^{-1}\sinh(aT)
we have used the inertial version of the current (
2.14
)
corresponding to the infinitely accelerated charge; this is because we have used inertial coordinates. In the case Rindler coordinates are more favorable, we
can use the right Rindler coordinates version inside this interval without problem, as both of these are describing the same object. One thing we need to be
careful is that
|t|<a^{-1}\sinh(aT)
implies
|\lambda|<T
in Rindler coordinates for this trajectory.

The compactification of the support is fairly straightforward. We consider the 4-current defined in Eqs. (
2.16
) as suddenly
appearing at a given point, and turn it off well after the particle has accelerated. To do this, let us define a free parameter
L
with the only condition that

L>a^{-1}\sinh(aT)
. The compactified 4-current is simply

j^{a}(x)=\tilde{j}^{a}_{\infty}(x)\,\theta(L-|t|),

(2.18)

where
\theta(x)
is the Heaviside step function
[
61
]
. This current has conservation problems, as
\nabla_{a}j^{a}\neq 0
at
t=\pm L
, which is remedied by taking the limit
L\to\infty
. This limit returns us to the physical situation described by the current
(
2.16
). On Fig.
2.1
the compactified current (
2.18
) is represented by the solid part of the
trajectory and the continuation of the trajectory by the dashed lines.

Given the retarded Green’s function for a point charge
q
following an arbitrary worldline
z^{a}(\tau)
, the retarded potential produced by this charge
is given by the Liénard-Wiechert potentials

[
54
,
2
]
, which in inertial coordinates are

Rj^{a}(x)=\frac{q}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{a}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}}(\tau^{\prime})\ \theta\,\bm{(}x^{0}-z^{0}(\tau^{\prime})\bm{)}\ \delta\,\bm{(}[x-z(\tau^{\prime})]^{2}\bm{)}\ \mathrm{d}\tau^{\prime}.

(2.19)

Born used this expression for the case where the charge is uniformly accelerated with uniform acceleration
a
for an infinite amount of
time
[
5
]
, i.e., following the trajectory of Eq. (
1.24
). This 4-potential in
inertial coordinates is given by

Rj^{t}(x)=\frac{q}{4\pi(t^{2}-z^{2})}\left[\frac{az(a^{-2}-t^{2}+r^{2})}{2\rho_{0}(x)}-t\right]\theta(t+z),

(2.20a)

Rj^{x}(x)=0,

(2.20b)

Rj^{y}(x)=0,

(2.20c)

Rj^{z}(x)=\frac{q}{4\pi(t^{2}-z^{2})}\left[\frac{at(a^{-2}-t^{2}+r^{2})}{2\rho_{0}(x)}-z\right]\theta(t+z),

(2.20d)

where
r^{2}=x^{2}+y^{2}+z^{2}
and
[
63
]

\rho_{0}(x)\coloneqq\frac{a}{2}\sqrt{\left(a^{-2}-t^{2}+r^{2}\right)^{2}+4a^{-2}(t^{2}-z^{2})}.

(2.21)

Whatever description we do on the classical setting, the field we find should coincide with this potential on the limit
T\to\infty
.

2.3
Extending Rindler and Unruh modes to a vector definition

We now proceed to define appropriate vector Rindler and Unruh modes to apply them to Maxwell electrodynamics. The solutions of the homogeneous field equation
(
2.11
) in the RRW can be decomposed in 4 polarization modes, taking advantage of the definition of scalar right Rindler modes. This was originally
reported in Ref.
[
19
]
as:

V_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\mathrm{R}(1)}=\frac{1}{k_{\perp}}(0,0,k_{y}v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},-k_{x}v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}),

(2.22a)

V_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\mathrm{R}(2)}=\frac{1}{k_{\perp}}(\partial_{\xi}v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},\partial_{\lambda}v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},0,0),

(2.22b)

V_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\mathrm{R}(G)}=\frac{1}{k_{\perp}}\nabla_{a}v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},

(2.22c)

V_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\mathrm{R}(L)}=\frac{1}{k_{\perp}}(0,0,k_{x}v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},k_{y}v^{\mathrm{R}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}).

(2.22d)

These have been selected in such a way that they are orthonormalized under the generalized Klein-Gordon inner product

\displaystyle\langle V_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\mathrm{R}(\kappa)},V_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}}^{\mathrm{R}(\kappa^{\prime})}\rangle_{\text{v}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\kappa\kappa^{\prime}}\,\delta(\omega-\omega^{\prime})\,\delta^{2}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}),

\displaystyle\langle V_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\mathrm{R}(\kappa)},\overline{V_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}}^{\mathrm{R}(\kappa^{\prime})}}\rangle_{\text{v}}

\displaystyle=0,

(2.23)

where
\kappa=1,2,G,L
. The labeling of the modes has been getting a little cumbersome but we can clarify it: The R indicates this is a right mode,
\omega
labels the energy carried by the mode as reported by the accelerated observer,
\mathbf{k}_{\perp}
is the corresponding transverse momentum in the
xy

plane, and the label in parenthesis corresponds with the polarization:
(1)
and
(2)
are the physical ones we will be dealing with,
(G)
is pure gauge and
does not carry information (also called non-physical), and
(L)
stands for longitudinal, which does not satisfy the Lorenz condition and therefore is also non-physical.

It is natural to extend the definitions of the electromagnetic right Rindler modes to the left
Rindler wedge as well, in a completely analogous fashion using left Rindler modes in left Rindler coordinates:

V_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\mathrm{L}(1)}=\frac{1}{k_{\perp}}(0,0,k_{y}v^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},-k_{x}v^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}),

(2.24a)

V_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\mathrm{L}(2)}=\frac{1}{k_{\perp}}(\partial_{\bar{\xi}}v^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},\partial_{\bar{\lambda}}v^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},0,0),

(2.24b)

V_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\mathrm{L}(G)}=\frac{1}{k_{\perp}}\nabla_{a}v^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},

(2.24c)

V_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\mathrm{L}(L)}=\frac{1}{k_{\perp}}(0,0,k_{x}v^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},k_{y}v^{\mathrm{L}}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}),

(2.24d)

definitions which in turn allow for the extension of the Unruh modes to the electromagnetic case in an analogous fashion to the scalar Unruh mode definition
(
1.47
)

\displaystyle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\,b}^{1(\kappa)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{V^{\mathrm{R}(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\,b}+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\omega/a}\overline{V^{\mathrm{L}(\kappa)}_{\omega\,-\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\,b}}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2\pi\omega/a}}},

\displaystyle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\,b}^{2(\kappa)}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{V^{\mathrm{L}(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\,b}+\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\omega/a}\overline{V^{\mathrm{R}(\kappa)}_{\omega\,-\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\,b}}}{\sqrt{1-e^{-2\pi\omega/a}}},

(2.25)

where
a
is the acceleration parameter of the coordinate transformation (
1.19
). These have been defined in such a
way that they inherit the orthonormality of the scalar Unruh modes and the electromagnetic Rindler modes [see Eqs. (
1.48
)
and (
2.23
)], i.e.,

\displaystyle\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},W_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}}^{\sigma^{\prime}(\kappa^{\prime})}\rangle_{\text{v}}

\displaystyle=\delta_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}\,\delta_{\kappa\kappa^{\prime}}\,\delta(\omega-\omega^{\prime})\,\delta^{2}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}),

\displaystyle\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},\overline{W_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp}}^{\sigma^{\prime}(\kappa^{\prime})}}\rangle_{\text{v}}

\displaystyle=0,

(2.26)

meaning the electromagnetic Unruh modes form a complete set of solutions for the
homogeneous electromagnetic field equation (
2.11
)
on the entirety of Minkowski spacetime
.

Another way to write the vector Unruh modes (
2.25
), after some simple calculations, is in terms of inertial coordinates. This is convenient
as Unruh modes are positive energy with regards to inertial time
t
. We find

\displaystyle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\sigma(1)}=\frac{1}{k_{\perp}}(0,k_{y}w^{\sigma}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},-k_{x}w^{\sigma}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},0),

(2.27a)

\displaystyle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\sigma(2)}=\frac{1}{k_{\perp}}(\nabla_{z}w^{\sigma}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},0,0,\nabla_{t}w^{\sigma}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}),

(2.27b)

\displaystyle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\sigma(G)}=\frac{1}{k_{\perp}}\nabla_{a}w^{\sigma}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},

(2.27c)

\displaystyle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\sigma(L)}=\frac{1}{k_{\perp}}(0,k_{x}w^{\sigma}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},k_{y}w^{\sigma}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},0),

(2.27d)

which are also useful to rewrite the vector Unruh modes in any other coordinate chart when needed.

One could also argue, although naively, that another appropriate selection of Unruh modes for the electromagnetic field could be given by

\tilde{W}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\,b}^{\sigma(\mu)}(x)=\epsilon^{(\mu)}_{b}w_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma}(x),

(2.28)

where the polarization vectors
\epsilon^{(\mu)}_{b}
are the same that the ones Ref.
[
64
]
uses for the plane wave
expansion. In inertial coordinates
(t,x,y,z)
, these are given by

\displaystyle\epsilon^{(1)}_{b}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1,0,0,1),

\displaystyle\epsilon^{(2)}_{b}

\displaystyle=(0,1,0,0),

\displaystyle\epsilon^{(3)}_{b}

\displaystyle=(0,0,1,0),

\displaystyle\epsilon^{(4)}_{b}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(1,0,0,-1).

(2.29)

Independently of how much more simple the modes (
2.28
) are, and even thought they are solutions to the homogeneous electromagnetic field equation
(
2.11
), none of these satisfies the Lorenz condition, meaning they are not physical solutions to the homogeneous electromagnetic field
equation.

2.4
Classical radiation emitted by the charge

For a point charge, the energy radiated over a unit of solid angle per emission time, measured far away from the source, can be found using the Poynting vector

\mathbf{S}
and the formula
[
2
,
54
]

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}H}{\mathrm{d}\Omega\,\mathrm{d}t_{\text{ret}}}=\lim_{R\to\infty}[1-\mathbf{v}(t_{\text{ret}})\cdot\mathbf{n}(t_{\text{ret}})]R^{2}\mathbf{S}(t)\cdot\mathbf{n}(t_{\text{ret}})\\
=\frac{q^{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\left(\frac{|\mathbf{n}(t_{\text{ret}})\times[\{\mathbf{n}(t_{\text{ret}})-\mathbf{v}(t_{\text{ret}})\}\times\mathbf{a}(t_{\text{ret}})]|^{2}}{[1-\mathbf{v}(t_{\text{ret}})\cdot\mathbf{n}(t_{\text{ret}})]^{5}}\right),

(2.30)

where
\mathbf{n}
is a unitary vector pointing along the line that joins the position of the charge at the instant when evaluated with the current measurement
point,
\mathbf{v}
is the velocity of the charge,
\mathbf{a}
is its acceleration,
R
is the distance between the charge and the measurement point in a
determined instant and
t_{\mathrm{ret}}=t-R
is the retarded time it takes the influence of the charge to reach the measuring point, meaning the
measurement is influenced only by events in the causal past. From here we see that the only causal contribution to radiated energy from a point charge come from
the parts of the trajectory where acceleration is nonzero. Let us write this a little more explicitly; we have
\mathbf{n}=(\sin\theta\cos\varphi,\sin\theta\sin\varphi,\cos\theta)
where the angles
\theta
and
\varphi
are defined as taking the point of emission as the origin
using spherical coordinates, while the velocity and acceleration of the charge described by current Eq. (
2.16
) are respectively
given by

\mathbf{v}(t)=\begin{cases}\bm{(}0,0,-\tanh(aT)\bm{)}&\text{if $t\leq-a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$},\\
\bm{(}0,0,at(1+a^{2}t^{2})^{-1/2}\bm{)}&\text{if $|t|<a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$},\\
\bm{(}0,0,\tanh(aT)\bm{)}&\text{if $t\geq a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$},\end{cases}

(2.31a)

\mathbf{a}(t)=\begin{cases}(0,0,0)&\text{if $|t|\geq a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$},\\
\bm{(}0,0,a(1+a^{2}t^{2})^{-3/2}\bm{)}&\text{if $|t|<a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$}.\end{cases}

(2.31b)

Thus, after some algebra, Eq. (
2.30
) reduces to

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}H}{\mathrm{d}\Omega\,\mathrm{d}t_{\text{ret}}}=\begin{cases}\frac{q^{2}}{16\pi^{2}}\left(\frac{a^{2}\sin^{2}\theta}{(1+a^{2}t_{\text{ret}}^{2})^{1/2}[(1+a^{2}t_{\text{ret}}^{2})^{1/2}-at_{\text{ret}}\cos\theta]^{5}}\right)&\text{if $|t_{\text{ret}}|<a^{-1}\sinh(aT)$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(2.32)

The condition
|t_{\text{ret}}|<a^{-1}\sinh(aT)
defines a spacetime volume: the causal future of the support of the accelerated part of the current.
We argue that to study the radiation emitted by an accelerated charge during a finite amount of time, it is sufficient to consider as a source

only the accelerated portion of the current
(
2.18
), since the inertial portions of the motion of the charge will not contribute
to the radiation emitted (nor detected) from it.

In the previous sections we have provided a reasonable model describing the accelerating charge, that accommodates to standard physical principles (i.e.,
continuity), but as we are interested in the description of radiation, it makes sense to, for the time being, disregard the inertial parts of the motion, as no
radiation will be coming from them. For completeness we will also present the correction terms for our calculations corresponding to the inertial parts.

Figure 2.2:
Conformal diagram of the situation showcasing the accelerated trajectory.

In red the accelerated part of the trajectory of the charge, in orange the Killing horizons, and in blue the Cauchy surfaces.

We consider two Cauchy surfaces in the spacetime, namely
\Sigma_{+}
and
\Sigma_{-}
, where
\Sigma_{+}
is on the chronological future of
\Sigma_{-}
and
neither of these surfaces are crossed by the accelerated part of the worldline of the charge. A schematic illustration of this situation is presented on
Fig.
2.2
. In the inertial frame,
using inertial coordinates
, the advanced and retarded solutions of the forced field Eq. (
