2.2a
) are respectively given by

\displaystyle Aj^{a}(x)=\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}G_{\text{adv}}(x,x^{\prime})\,j^{a}(x^{\prime}),

(2.33a)

\displaystyle Rj^{a}(x)=\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}G_{\text{ret}}(x,x^{\prime})\,j^{a}(x^{\prime}).

(2.33b)

Here
G_{\text{adv}}
and
G_{\text{ret}}
are the advanced and retarded Green’s functions for the scalar wave
equation
[
2
]
. We can define the regularized solution

Ej^{a}\coloneqq Aj^{a}-Rj^{a}.

(2.34)

From this definition we can see that the regularized solution coincides with the advanced one for all events in the past Cauchy surface [
Aj^{a}(x)=Ej^{a}(x)

if
x\in\Sigma_{-}
], and in the future surface the regularized solution is simply given by the contrary of the retarded solution [
Rj^{a}(x)=-Ej^{a}(x)
for
x\in\Sigma_{+}
]. This is due to the structure of the definition of
Ej^{a}
: in the future Cauchy surface only the current on the
causal past of
\Sigma_{+}
contributes to this function, provided that the trajectory of the charge does not cross this Cauchy surface. The reasoning is
analogous for
\Sigma_{-}
.

The next step is to expand the retarded solution of Eq. (
2.2a
) in terms of vector Unruh modes. On the future Cauchy surface
\Sigma_{+}
we
have

Rj_{a}(x)=-\sum_{\sigma=1}^{2}\sum_{\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\Bigg[\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}\,W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\sigma(\kappa)}(x)\\
+\overline{\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}}\,\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\sigma(\kappa)}(x)}\Bigg],

(2.35)

where
\kappa=1,2,G,L
labels the polarization of each mode, and the inner products
\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}
and
\overline{\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}}
corresponds with the coefficients of the regularized
solution. To find the coefficients we can generalize one of the Lemmas of Ref.
[
65
]
(what we from now on call
the Wald identity
) for the electromagnetic field in the
Feynman gauge by taking advantage of the fact that it is only dependent on the choice of Cauchy surfaces, the inner product used, the field equation, and the
compactly supported source.

2.4.1
Coefficients of the expansion

We can begin by considering a globally hyperbolic
spacetime
M
with metric
g_{ab}
. Let
j_{a}
be the components of a compact supported 4-current, source of the electromagnetic field like in
Eq. (
2.2a
). Let also
{A}_{a}
be a solution of the homogeneous field equation for the electromagnetic potential
(
2.11
). Then we can find that the regularized solution (
2.34
) corresponding to this current satisfies

\langle{A},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}=-\mathrm{i}\iiiint_{M}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\sqrt{-g(x)}\,\overline{{A}_{a}(x)}\,j^{a}(x),

(2.36)

which allows us to find the coefficients of the expansion (
2.35
) rather quickly. It is important to stress the fact that this identity

is only valid when the current is compactly supported
, which is one of the key facts that allow for the derivation of this identity. This result is
further discussed and proven in section
A.1
of the appendix.

We notice that only the Unruh modes with polarization labeled by
2
and
G
couple with the accelerated part of the current (
2.18
).
Therefore, following the reasoning presented at the beginning of this section, we can compute the integrals (
2.36
) that correspond to the
coefficients that appear for the radiative part of the Unruh mode expansion,
5
5

5

See section
A.2
of the appendix for a
verification of these.

\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{1(2)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}=\mathcal{I}^{1(2)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}-\frac{2\mathrm{i}q}{\sqrt{1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\omega/a}}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{\sinh(\pi\omega/a)}{4\pi^{4}a}}\right]\mathrm{K}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{i}\omega/a}(k_{\perp}/a)\,\frac{\sin(\omega T)}{\omega},

(2.37a)

\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{1(G)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}=\mathcal{I}^{1(G)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}+\frac{2q\omega k_{\perp}^{-1}}{\sqrt{1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\omega/a}}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{\sinh(\pi\omega/a)}{4\pi^{4}a}}\right]\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{i}\omega/a}(k_{\perp}/a)\,\frac{\sin(\omega T)}{\omega},

(2.37b)

\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{2(2)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}=\mathcal{I}^{2(2)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}-\frac{2\mathrm{i}q\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\omega/a}}{\sqrt{1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\omega/a}}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{\sinh(\pi\omega/a)}{4\pi^{4}a}}\right]\mathrm{K}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{i}\omega/a}(k_{\perp}/a)\,\frac{\sin(\omega T)}{\omega},

(2.37c)

\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{2(G)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}=\mathcal{I}^{2(G)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}-\frac{2q\omega k_{\perp}^{-1}\mathrm{e}^{-\pi\omega/a}}{\sqrt{1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\pi\omega/a}}}\left[\sqrt{\frac{\sinh(\pi\omega/a)}{4\pi^{4}a}}\right]\mathrm{K}_{\mathrm{i}\omega/a}(k_{\perp}/a)\,\frac{\sin(\omega T)}{\omega},

(2.37d)

where
\mathcal{I}^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}
are the contributions due to the inertial part of the motion. These are carefully analyzed in
section
A.3
of the appendix to show no spurious divergencies arise from the compactification of the support; however we need the
result that when the charge accelerates for an infinite amount of time these do not contribute to the description of the field, i.e.,

\lim_{T\to\infty}\mathcal{I}^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}=0,

(2.38)

an expected result since there is no inertial part of the motion in this situation.

Let us now consider the case when the charge accelerates for an infinite amount of time:
T\to\infty
. Here the current is conserved:
\nabla_{a}j^{a}=0
and, as we saw, the correction terms do not appear at all. For the coefficients (
2.37
) on
this limit we can use the result

\lim_{T\to\infty}\frac{\sin(\omega T)}{\omega}=\pi\delta(\omega)

(2.39)

and the identity
[
61
,
66
,
67
]

\frac{\partial{}}{\partial{z}}\mathrm{K}_{\nu}(z)=\frac{\nu}{z}\mathrm{K}_{\nu}(z)+\mathrm{K}_{\nu+1}(z),

(2.40)

for the modified Bessel functions of the second kind, thus finding

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to\infty}\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{1(2)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}=\lim_{T\to\infty}\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{2(2)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}=\frac{\mathrm{i}q}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{2}{a}}\mathrm{K}_{1}(k_{\perp}/a)\delta(\omega),

(2.41a)

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to\infty}\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{1(G)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}=\lim_{T\to\infty}\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{2(G)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}=0,

(2.41b)

results which we will heavily depend on.

The positive energy part of the retarded solution of (
2.2a
) is the complex-valued 4-vector

KRj_{a}(x)\coloneqq-\sum_{\kappa,\sigma}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\langle W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}(x),

(2.42)

made up of a superposition of positive energy terms only. This is useful to define a total number of classical inertial photons as
N_{\text{cU}}\coloneqq\langle KRj,KRj\rangle_{\text{v}}
. We can write this number explicitly by taking advantage of the orthonormality of the Unruh modes (
2.26
), from
where we find

N_{\text{cU}}=+\sum_{\kappa,\sigma}\sum_{\kappa^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}\omega\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}\omega^{\prime}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}^{\prime}\\
\times\overline{\langle W^{\sigma^{\prime}(\kappa^{\prime})}_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}^{\prime}},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}}\langle W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}\,\langle W^{\sigma^{\prime}(\kappa^{\prime})}_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}^{\prime}},W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}\rangle_{\text{v}}\\
=\sum_{\kappa,\sigma}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\left|\langle W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}\right|^{2},

(2.43)

meaning we can interpret the coefficient
|\langle W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}|^{2}
as the number of photons associated to the Unruh mode
W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}
. To avoid divergences we can deal with the number of Unruh photons per transverse momentum

\mathrm{d}N_{\text{cU}}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp})=\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\sum_{\kappa,\sigma}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\,\left|\langle W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}\right|^{2}.

(2.44)

This can be computed for the amplitudes found in Eqs. (
2.37
) and their corresponding corrections; however difficult these
integrals might be. On the limit
T\to\infty
we can use the coefficients (
2.41
), and the property
W^{2(2)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}=W^{1(2)}_{-\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}
, directly inherited from the scalar Unruh modes
[
52
]
, to see
that

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}N_{\text{cU}}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\,\left|\langle W^{2(2)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}\right|^{2}=\frac{q^{2}}{2\pi^{2}a}|\mathrm{K}_{1}(k_{\perp}/a)|^{2}\delta(\omega)|_{\omega=0}

\displaystyle=\frac{q^{2}}{4\pi^{3}a}\,|\mathrm{K}_{1}(k_{\perp}/a)|^{2}\,T_{\text{tot}}\,\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp},

(2.45)

where we have used the standard interpretation
T_{\text{tot}}=2\pi\delta(\omega)|_{\omega=0}
. This result is the same as the total rate of emission and
absorption of zero-energy Rindler photons in the Unruh thermal bath seen by an accelerated observer, and the rate of emission detected by an inertial one, as
reported by Ref.
[
19
]
where tree-level quantum field theory was used.

For consistency’s sake, it is useful to show that the expansion (
2.35
) reduces to the usual and well known solution for the
electromagnetic 4-potential (
2.20
) in the EDKU. On the limit
T\to\infty
we can see from
Eq. (
2.41
) that the only modes that couple with our current are
W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\sigma(2)}
. Then we
have, by using again that
W^{2(2)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}=W^{1(2)}_{-\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}
, that

\displaystyle Rj_{a}(x)

\displaystyle=-\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\left(\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{2(2)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}\,W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{2(2)}{}_{a}(x)+\langle\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{2(2)},Ej}\rangle_{\text{v}}\,\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{2(2)}{}_{a}(x)}\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\mathrm{i}q}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{2}{a}}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\left[W_{0\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{2(2)}{}_{a}(x)-\overline{W_{0\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{2(2)}{}_{a}(x)}\right]\mathrm{K}_{1}(k_{\perp}/a).

(2.46)

On the EDKU, we know Rindler coordinates are given by (
1.19b
), and the vector Unruh mode of interest reads

W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{2(2)}=k_{\perp}^{-1}(\partial_{\eta}w^{2}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},\partial_{\zeta}w^{2}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}},0,0)
,
while the scalar Unruh mode is given by

w^{2}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}(\eta,\zeta,\mathbf{x}_{\perp})=-\mathrm{i}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\pi\omega/(2a)}}{\sqrt{32\pi^{2}a}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{\perp}+\omega\zeta)}\mathrm{H}^{(2)}_{\mathrm{i}\omega/a}(k_{\perp}\mathrm{e}^{a\eta}/a).

(2.47)

From here we see that as
\partial_{\zeta}w^{2}_{0\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}=0
, the only non-zero component of the 4-potential will be the one along the foliation curves
of
\zeta
. When writing this component we will need the property

\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}z}\mathrm{H}^{(2)}_{0}(z)=-\mathrm{H}^{(2)}_{1}(z).

(2.48)

Also, the dot product in the exponential is given by
\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\cdot\mathbf{x}_{\perp}={k}_{\perp}x_{\perp}\cos\vartheta
, where
\vartheta
is the angle that separated
these two vectors; from this we see that we can use “polar coordinates”
(k_{\perp},\vartheta)\in[0,\infty)\times[0,2\pi)
, with
\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}=k_{\perp}\mathrm{d}k_{\perp}\mathrm{d}\vartheta
, to carry out the integration of the transverse momentum (as we consider
\mathbf{x}_{\perp}
as “fixed” while calculating the
4-potential). Following this outline, we find that the nonzero component of the 4-potential is

Rj_{\zeta}(x)=\frac{q\mathrm{e}^{a\eta}}{8\pi^{2}a}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}{k}_{\perp}\,k_{\perp}\,\mathrm{K}_{1}(k_{\perp}/a)\left[\mathrm{H}^{(2)}_{1}(k_{\perp}\mathrm{e}^{a\eta}/a)\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}\vartheta\ \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k_{\perp}x_{\perp}\cos\vartheta}\right.\\
\left.+\ \overline{\mathrm{H}^{(2)}_{1}(k_{\perp}\mathrm{e}^{a\eta}/a)}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}\vartheta\ \mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}k_{\perp}x_{\perp}\cos\vartheta}\right].

(2.49)

We can now use the integral form of the Bessel function of order 0

\mathrm{J}_{0}(z)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}z\cos\vartheta}\ \mathrm{d}\vartheta,

(2.50)

its evenness:
\mathrm{J}_{0}(-z)=\mathrm{J}_{0}(z)
, and the definition of the second Hankel function
\mathrm{H}^{(2)}_{1}(z)=\mathrm{J}_{1}(z)-\mathrm{i}\mathrm{Y}_{1}(z)
, to find the integral expression

Rj_{\zeta}(x)=\frac{q\mathrm{e}^{a\eta}}{2\pi a}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}{k}_{\perp}\,k_{\perp}\,\mathrm{K}_{1}(k_{\perp}/a)\ \mathrm{J}_{1}(k_{\perp}\mathrm{e}^{a\eta}/a)\ \mathrm{J}_{0}(k_{\perp}x_{\perp}).

(2.51)

From the identity
[
51
,
68
]

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\vartheta\ \vartheta\ \mathrm{K}_{1}(\alpha\vartheta)\,\mathrm{J}_{1}(\mathrm{i}\beta\vartheta)\,\mathrm{J}_{0}(\gamma\vartheta)=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\alpha\beta}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}+\gamma^{2}}{\sqrt{(\alpha^{2}+\beta^{2}+\gamma^{2})^{2}-4\alpha^{2}\beta^{2}}}-1\right),

(2.52)

valid when
\mathrm{Re}\,\alpha>0
,
\mathrm{Re}\,\beta=0
and
\gamma>0
we see that the the components of the 4-potential are

\displaystyle Rj_{\zeta}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{qa}{4\pi}\left(\frac{a^{-2}-a^{-2}\mathrm{e}^{2a\eta}+x_{\perp}^{2}}{\sqrt{(a^{-2}-a^{-2}\mathrm{e}^{2a\eta}+x_{\perp}^{2})^{2}+4a^{-4}\mathrm{e}^{2a\eta}}}-1\right),

\displaystyle Rj_{\eta}

\displaystyle=Rj_{x}=Rj_{y}=0.

(2.53)

The Born solution (
2.20
) written in Rindler coordinates for the EDKU is physically equivalent to this result. This can be made
apparent by applying the gauge transformation
Rj_{b}\to\widetilde{Rj}_{b}=Rj_{b}-[aq/(4\pi)]\nabla_{b}(\zeta+\eta)
directly.

2.5
Quantum analysis using Unruh modes

If we consider the Lagrangian density defined from the action of Eq. (
2.7
) to describe the dynamics of the quantum electromagnetic 4-potential
\hat{A}^{a}
, the
resulting Euler-Lagrange equation, in the Feynman gauge
\alpha=1
, are

\nabla_{b}\nabla^{b}\hat{A}_{a}=j_{a}\hat{\mathbb{1}},

(2.54)

where
j^{a}
is the classical compactly supported current of Eq. (
2.18
). We can focus on two cases to write this 4-potential:

•

describing the field in the Cauchy surface
\Sigma_{-}\penalty\ 4
, which lies outside of the causal future of the support of
j_{a}
:
\hat{A}_{a}=\hat{A}^{\text{in}}_{a}+Rj_{a}\hat{\mathbb{1}}
, where
\hat{A}^{\text{in}}_{a}
is the solution to the homogeneous field equation, i.e.,
\nabla_{b}\nabla^{b}\hat{A}^{\text{in}}_{a}=0
. From this we can propose the expansion

\hat{A}^{\text{in}}_{a}(t,\mathbf{x})=\sum_{j}\left[u_{(j)\,a}(t,\mathbf{x})\hat{a}_{\text{in}}(\overline{u_{(j)}})+\overline{u_{(j)\,a}(t,\mathbf{x})}\hat{a}_{\text{in}}^{\dagger}(u_{(j)})\right],

(2.55)

where
\{u_{(j)}\}
is any set of positive energy modes in the entirety of Minkowski spacetime, i.e., solutions of the homogeneous field equation (
2.11
). We
can define
|0^{M}_{\text{in}}\rangle
as the state such that
\hat{a}_{\text{in}}(\overline{u_{(j)}})|0^{M}_{\text{in}}\rangle=0
, for any mode labeled with

j
. As we used the classical retarded solution when writing the complete solution for the 4-potential, we can identify
|0^{M}_{\text{in}}\rangle
as the
vacuum seen by an inertial observer on the (asymptotic) causal future of the support of
j_{a}
. We can construct the Fock space describing the state of the
field by successive applications of the
creation operators

\hat{a}_{\text{in}}^{\dagger}(u_{(j)})
, meaning that an arbitrary state where each mode

j
has
n_{j}
particles can be written as

|\text{in:}n_{j_{1}},n_{j_{2}},\cdots\rangle=\bigotimes_{j=j_{1}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}!}}\left[\hat{a}_{\text{in}}^{\dagger}(u_{(j)})\right]^{n_{j}}|0^{M}_{\text{in}}\rangle,

(2.56)

•

describing the field in the Cauchy surface
\Sigma_{+}
, which lies outside of the causal past of the support of
j_{a}
:
\hat{A}_{a}=\hat{A}^{\text{out}}_{a}+Aj_{a}\hat{\mathbb{1}}
, where
\hat{A}^{\text{out}}_{a}
is the solution to the homogeneous field equation, i.e.,
\nabla_{b}\nabla^{b}\hat{A}^{\text{out}}_{a}=0
. From this we can propose the expansion

\hat{A}^{\text{out}}_{a}(t,\mathbf{x})=\sum_{j}\left[v_{(j)\,a}(t,\mathbf{x})\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{v_{(j)}})+\overline{v_{(j)\,a}(t,\mathbf{x})}\hat{a}_{\text{out}}^{\dagger}(v_{(j)})\right],

(2.57)

where
\{v_{(j)}\}
is any set of positive energy modes in the entirety of Minkowski spacetime, as it solves the homogeneous field equation (
2.11
). We can define
|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{out}}\rangle
as the
state such that
\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{v_{(j)}})|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{out}}\rangle=0
, for any mode labeled with
j
. As we used the classical advanced
solution when writing the complete solution for the 4-potential, we can identify
|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{out}}\rangle
as the vacuum seen by an inertial observer on
the (asymptotic) causal future of the support of
j_{a}
. We can construct the Fock space describing the state of the field by successive applications of
the
creation operators

\hat{a}_{\text{out}}^{\dagger}(v_{(j)})
, meaning that an arbitrary state where each mode
j
has
n_{j}
particles
can be written as

|\text{out:}n_{j_{1}},n_{j_{2}},\cdots\rangle=\bigotimes_{j=j_{1}}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n_{j}!}}\left[\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(v_{(j)})\right]^{n_{j}}|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{out}}\rangle.

(2.58)

2.5.1
The S matrix

The basic idea that is the base of the following analysis is to connect both of these descriptions.
For this purpose we can define the S matrix
[
64
]
by

\hat{S}\coloneqq\exp\left[-\mathrm{i}\iiiint_{M}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\hat{A}^{\text{out}}_{a}(t,\mathbf{x})j^{a}(t,\mathbf{x})\right],

(2.59)

which relates both vacuums by
|0^{M}_{\text{in}}\rangle=\hat{S}|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{out}}\rangle
. To find this matrix in terms of the out-creation and out-annihilation
operators let’s expand the out-field using Unruh modes:

\hat{A}^{\text{out}}_{a}(x)=\sum_{\sigma=1}^{2}\sum_{\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\left[W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\sigma(\kappa)}(x)\,\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}})+\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\sigma(\kappa)}(x)}\,\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)})\right].

(2.60)

From this, we can define the smearing of the 4-potential with the current as:

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}\hat{A}^{\text{out}}(j)

\displaystyle\coloneqq-\mathrm{i}\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\,\hat{A}^{\text{out}}_{a}(x)j^{a}(x),

(2.61)

which can be expanded:

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\hat{A}^{\text{out}}(j)

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{i}\sum_{\sigma,\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\!\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\left\{\left[\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\sqrt{-g}\,j^{a}(x)W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\sigma(\kappa)}(x)\right]\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}})+\text{H.c.}\right\},

(2.62)

where
H.c.
stands for the
Hermitian conjugate
of the term on the left. We can now recognize the terms inside the square brackets as the
generalized Klein-Gordon inner product of identity (
2.36
), and defining the
total creation operator
associated to the (inertial) positive
energy part of the expansion

\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(KEj)\coloneqq\sum_{\sigma,\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}\,\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}),

(2.63)

and the
total annihilation operator
associated to the (inertial) positive
energy part of the expansion

\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{KEj})\coloneqq\sum_{\sigma,\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\overline{\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}}\,\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}}),

(2.64)

we can simplify the S matrix (
2.59
) as

\hat{S}=\exp\left[\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(KEj)-\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{KEj})\right].

(2.65)

We can further develop equation (
2.65
) by using the Zassenhaus formula

\exp(\hat{X}+\hat{Y})=\exp(\hat{X})\,\exp(\hat{Y})\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}[\hat{X},\hat{Y}]\right),

(2.66)

valid when the operators
\hat{X}
and
\hat{Y}
satisfy
\bm{[}\hat{X},[\hat{X},\hat{Y}]\bm{]}=\bm{[}\hat{Y},[\hat{X},\hat{Y}]\bm{]}=0
, and explicitly computing the commutator
[\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(KEj),-\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{KEj})]

by using the canonical commutation relation

[\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W_{\omega^{\prime}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}^{\prime}}^{\sigma^{\prime}(\kappa^{\prime})}}),\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)})]=\delta_{\sigma\sigma^{\prime}}\,\delta_{\kappa\kappa^{\prime}}\,\delta(\omega-\omega^{\prime})\,\delta^{2}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}_{\perp})\,\hat{\mathbb{1}},

(2.67)

obtained from the normalization of the vector Unruh modes (
2.26
) [see also
Eq. (3.21) of Ref.
[
19
]
from where this equation can also be deduced], to show the
S matrix can be written as

\hat{S}=\mathrm{e}^{-\|KEj\|^{2}/2}\exp[\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(KEj)]\exp[-\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{KEj})].

(2.68)

From here we can find a problem with this method, noticing that the modulus of the positive part of
Ej
is

\|KEj\|^{2}=\sum_{\sigma,\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\left|\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}\right|^{2},

(2.69)

which will be divergent, as we are summing up all transverse momentum, even on the limit
6
6

6

This means
\mathrm{e}^{-\|KEj\|^{2}/2}\to 0
. However, as
the rest of the operator compounding the S matrix are divergent (producing non-normalizable vectors), the net result is that the S matrix is well defined.

T\to\infty
.
This form of the S matrix is useful to find the action of
\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}})
over the in-vacuum,
which in turn will be of use to find the expectation values of the electromagnetic field and the energy-momentum tensor.

Let us procede with this calculation. First, from Eq. (
2.68
),
we have

|0^{M}_{\text{in}}\rangle=\mathrm{e}^{-\|KEj\|^{2}/2}\exp[\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(KEj)]|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{out}}\rangle,

(2.70)

which is a direct consequence of the definition of the exponential of the annihilation operator. We can also apply
\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}})
to
Eq. (
2.70
). After some simple algebra

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}})|0^{M}_{\text{in}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{-\|KEj\|^{2}/2}\mathrm{e}^{\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(KEj)}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(KEj)}\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}})\mathrm{e}^{\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(KEj)}\right)|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{out}}\rangle.

(2.71)

We can now use the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff formula

\mathrm{e}^{-\hat{X}}\hat{Y}\mathrm{e}^{\hat{X}}=\hat{Y}-[\hat{X},\hat{Y}]+\frac{1}{2!}\bm{[}\hat{X},[\hat{X},\hat{Y}]\bm{]}+\ldots,

(2.72)

and study of the commutator
[\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(KEj),\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}})]
which, after
using the canonical commutation relation (
2.67
), can be straightforwardly shown to be

[\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(KEj),\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}})]=-\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}\hat{\mathbb{1}}.

(2.73)

Combining these facts we find that the in-vacuum is an eigenstate of the annihilation operator
associated to any Unruh mode

\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}})|0^{M}_{\text{in}}\rangle=\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}|0^{M}_{\text{in}}\rangle,

(2.74)

i.e., a multimode coherent state. Notice how this property is independent of the duration of the acceleration. We can use this to find the expectation value
of the out-potential (
2.60
) in the in-vacuum

\langle 0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}|\hat{A}^{\text{out}}_{a}|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}\rangle\\
=\sum_{\sigma,\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\mathrm{d}\omega\!\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\!\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\!\left[W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\sigma(\kappa)}\,\langle 0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}|\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}})|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}\rangle+\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\ a}^{\sigma(\kappa)}}\,\langle 0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}|\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)})|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}\rangle\right]\\
=Ej(x)=-Rj(x),

(2.75)

the classical retarded solution for the 4-potential as the out-field is defined in the future Cauchy surface
\Sigma_{+}
. This implies that the
out-field has an expectation value given by

\displaystyle\langle 0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}|\hat{F}^{\text{out}}_{ab}|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle 0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}|(\nabla_{a}\hat{A}^{\text{out}}_{b}-\nabla_{b}\hat{A}^{\text{out}}_{a})|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}\rangle=-RF_{ab},

(2.76)

where
RF_{ab}=\nabla_{a}Rj_{b}-\nabla_{b}Rj_{a}
is the classical
Faraday tensor obtained from the retarded potential.

We can now study the normal ordered stress-energy tensor for the out-field

:\!\hat{T}^{ab}_{\text{out}}\!:\,=g_{cd}\!:\!\hat{F}^{ac}_{\text{out}}\hat{F}^{bd}_{\text{out}}\!:-\frac{1}{4}g^{ab}\!:\!\hat{F}^{cd}_{\text{out}}\hat{F}_{cd}^{\text{out}}\!:.

(2.77)

Thanks to the normal ordering and as the in-vacuum is a multimode coherent state, we find by straightforward computation that the expectation value of the normal ordered stress-energy tensor is

\langle 0^{M}_{\text{in}}|:\!\hat{T}^{ab}_{\text{out}}\!:|0^{M}_{\text{in}}\rangle\,=g_{cd}RF^{ac}RF^{bd}-\frac{1}{4}g^{ab}RF^{cd}RF_{cd}=T^{ab}[RF],

(2.78)

the classical value of the stress-energy momentum tensor for the retarded field.

2.5.2
The role of zero-energy Rindler photons

In order to show this role, we need to avoid the divergence of Eq. (
2.69
). Let us define the creation and annihilation operator per transverse momentum respectively by:

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp})\coloneqq\sum_{\sigma,\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}{\omega}\,\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}\,\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}),

(2.79)

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp})\coloneqq\sum_{\sigma,\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}{\omega}\,\overline{\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}}\ \hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}}),

(2.80)

from where we see that the total creation and annihilation operators associated to the positive (negative) energy of the expansion, defined in Eqs. (
2.63
) and (
2.64
),
are related to these by

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(KEj)

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\,\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp}),

and

\displaystyle\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{KEj})

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\,\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp}).

(2.81)

Then the S matrix can be written as

\hat{S}=\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\sigma,\kappa}\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}{\omega}\left|\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}\right|^{2}\right)\\
\times\exp\left(\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp})\right)\exp\left(-\iint_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp})\right),

(2.82)

which in turn allows us to use the Zassenhaus formula for each of the exponentials, remembering the annihilation (creation) operators

\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)}})
[
\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)})
] commute with each
other for different values of
\mathbf{k}_{\perp}
. These can be
rearranged into a single multimode expression, thus, if this is
applied over the out-vacuum we obtain

|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}\rangle=\hat{S}|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{out}}\rangle=\bigotimes_{\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\sigma,\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}{\omega}\left|\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}\right|^{2}\right)\mathrm{e}^{\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp})}|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{out}}\rangle,

(2.83)

as
\mathrm{e}^{-\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp})}|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{out}}\rangle=|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{out}}\rangle
for all
\mathbf{k}_{\perp}
,
\omega
,

\sigma
and
\kappa
. Let us write Eq. (
2.83
)
in a more explicit fashion for the limit
T\to\infty
. We can use the coefficients (
2.41
) and the result
(
2.45
) to find the integral inside the exponential:

\sum_{\sigma,\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}{\omega}\,|\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}|^{2}=\frac{q^{2}}{4\pi^{3}a}\left|\mathrm{K}_{1}(k_{\perp}/a)\right|^{2}T_{\text{tot}}.

(2.84)

On the other hand, using
\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{1(2)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}=\langle W_{-\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{2(2)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}
, the creation operator per transverse momentum (
2.79
) is easily rewritten as

\displaystyle\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp})

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{i}q}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{2}{a}}\mathrm{K}_{1}(k_{\perp}/a)\,\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(W_{0\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{2(2)}),

(2.85)

From these we find that on the limit

T\to\infty
the in-vacuum (
2.83
) is given by

|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}\rangle=\bigotimes_{\mathbf{k}_{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-q^{2}|\mathrm{K}_{1}(k_{\perp}/a)|^{2}T_{\text{tot}}/(8a\pi^{3})}\exp\left(\frac{\mathrm{i}q}{2\pi}\sqrt{\frac{2}{a}}\mathrm{K}_{1}(k_{\perp}/a)\,\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(W_{0\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{2(2)})\right)|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{out}}\rangle,

(2.86)

which corresponds to an entangled superposition of excited states of the out-vacuum formed exclusively by zero-energy Rindler photons.

We can also find the number of photons per Unruh mode in the future surface using the number operator
\hat{N}^{\text{qU}}_{\text{out}}(W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}})\coloneqq\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}})\,\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}})
, which has an
expectation value in the in-vacuum given by

\displaystyle\langle 0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}|\hat{N}_{\text{out}}(W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}})|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle 0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}|\hat{a}^{\dagger}_{\text{out}}(W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}})\,\hat{a}_{\text{out}}(\overline{W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}})|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}\rangle=|\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}|^{2}.

(2.87)

Notice how this expectation value naturally coincides with both the value and interpretation we gave the integrand of (
2.43
). We can now sum up for all polarizations
\kappa
, Unruh modes
\sigma
, and integrate them with respect to Rindler energy
\omega
to obtain the
number of photons per transverse momentum
\mathbf{k}_{\perp}
:

\displaystyle N_{\text{qU}}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{\sigma,\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\,\langle 0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}|\hat{N}_{\text{out}}(W^{\sigma(\kappa)}_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}})|0^{\mathrm{M}}_{\text{in}}\rangle=\sum_{\sigma,\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\omega\,|\langle W_{\omega\mathbf{k}_{\perp}}^{\sigma(\kappa)},Ej\rangle_{\text{v}}|^{2},

(2.88)

which we can easily compute for the
T\to\infty
case, where only
\omega=0
modes contribute, by using Eq. (
2.84
) to see that

N_{\text{qU}}(\mathbf{k}_{\perp})=\frac{q^{2}}{4\pi^{2}a}|\mathrm{K}_{1}(k_{\perp}/a)|^{2}T_{\text{tot}},

(2.89)

which is consistent with our previous result in the classical case (
2.45
) and thus,
also with the result obtained by

[
19
]

[
19
]
.

2.6
Partial discussion

This chapter is about the classical and quantum radiation emitted by a uniformly accelerated classical charge. We extended the definition of the well known
Unruh modes used in scalar electrodynamics to the vector case and used their properties relating inertial and accelerated observers to our advantage.

On the classical part of the study, we found the Unruh mode decomposition for the retarded potential, and the corresponding amplitudes for our current of
compact support that describes a point charge accelerated for a finite amount of proper time. After this, we extended it to the case where the acceleration time
is infinite to explicitly show that the only modes that construct the 4-potential have zero Rindler energy, and that a definition for the number of photons
is consistent with previous findings that used purely quantum methods. We were also successful in reconstructing the well-known electromagnetic field produced
by this charge from our mode decomposition.

For the quantum analysis we focused on connecting the observations of the asymptotic past (before the current is turned on) to the content of the asymptotic
future (after the current is turned off), using the vector Unruh modes for the description in the past. Using the S matrix, we find that the amplitudes of the
classical expansion connect both of the descriptions. Furthermore, if the state is prepared as the vacuum for the past inertial observer, the future ones will
detect that their vacuum is a multimode coherent state, and thus they will find that the expectation values of the 4-potential, the fields, and stress-energy
tensors are in agreement with the corresponding counterparts that arise form the retarded solution described in the classical analysis, independently of the
amount of proper time the charge is accelerated. Again we return to case when the proper time of the acceleration is infinite, to find that the particle content
described by future inertial observers is built entirely from zero-energy Rindler photons.

– Chapter 3 –
Gravitational radiation

Within the context of the General Relativity, the theory of classical gravitational perturbations has been extensively treated in literature, from where we can
mention references like the review by
[
69
]

[
69
]
and the book by
[
43
]

[
43
]
to understand the basics. On the
other hand, discussions on quantized gravitational waves were started by

\Citeauthor
rosenfeldUeberGravitationswirkungenLichtes1930
[
70
,
71
]
and

\Citeauthor
bronsteinRepublicationQuantumTheory2012
[
72
]
with modern works (e.g.
Refs.
[
37
,
73
]
) looking to shed light on our understanding of (weak) gravity as a
quantum field.

In this chapter we present the results of the second paper corresponding to this project
[
74
]
, which was recently
published. We proceed in similar fashion to the previous chapter, analyzing a point-like source, but this time we dedicate a considerable amount of effort
defining and describing the modes used for the perturbation. First we set the conventions and the basics of the theory that we will use.

3.1
The perturbation of spacetime

We will consider perturbations of the metric around a fixed background for which we will use the standard name
g_{ab}
, and the perturbations will be
called
h_{ab}=\delta g_{ab}
. Then, the full metric of the spacetime will be given by

g^{\mathrm{tot}}_{ab}=g_{ab}+h_{ab}.

(3.1)

Here we consider only small perturbations, i.e,
|h_{ab}|\ll 1
. Clearly, the background metric corresponds with the full metric when the perturbation is
zero:
h_{ab}=0
, and is not necessarily constant (like is the case of the Minkowski metric). The dynamics of the perturbation are governed by the linearized
Einstein field equations, which can be obtained from two separate but equivalent paths: (i) writing Einstein’s equation (
1.18
) up to
the first order of
h_{ab}
;
(ii) as the Euler-Lagrange equations for
h_{ab}
of the Hilbert-Einstein Lagrangian density, which is written up to the second order of perturbation.

We will use the second option with some added terms, but will omit the derivation as it can be found in literature
[
75
]
.
We postulate the total Lagrangian density of the system
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{\text{p}}+\mathcal{L}_{\text{matter}}
, where
\mathcal{L}_{\text{matter}}
is the non-gravitational, or matter part, and
\mathcal{L}_{\text{p}}
is the gravitational perturbation part, which is given by
(omitting the cosmological constant and mass terms)
[
75
,
76
]

\mathcal{L}_{\text{p}}=\mathcal{L}_{\text{inv}}+\mathcal{L}_{\text{gf}},

(3.2)

where the
invariant
part
1
1

1

We abbreviate
h\coloneqq g^{ab}h_{ab}
and we used the background metric to raise and lower indices.

\mathcal{L}_{\text{inv}}=-\frac{\sqrt{-g}}{4\kappa^{2}}\left[\nabla_{c}h_{ab}\nabla^{c}h^{ab}-\nabla_{c}h\nabla^{c}h+2\nabla_{a}h^{ab}\nabla_{b}h-2\nabla^{a}h_{ac}\nabla_{b}h^{bc}\right],

(3.3)

arises from the second order perturbation to the Hilbert-Einstein Lagrangian density
\mathcal{L}_{\text{HE}}=\sqrt{-g}R/\kappa^{2}
where
R
is the Ricci
scalar, and
\kappa^{2}=16\pi G
. The Lagrangian density (
3.3
) is invariant (and receives its name from this
fact) under the gauge transformation
h_{ab}\to h_{ab}-2\nabla_{(a}\Lambda_{b)}
, where
\Lambda_{a}
is an arbitrary vector field. The second term

\mathcal{L}_{\text{gf}}=\frac{\sqrt{-g}}{2\alpha\kappa^{2}}\left(\nabla^{a}h_{ab}-\frac{1+\beta}{\beta}\nabla_{b}h\right)\!\left(\nabla_{c}h^{cb}-\frac{1+\beta}{\beta}\nabla^{b}h\right)

(3.4)

is the gauge-fixing term used to eliminate the gauge freedoms. In all of this,
\nabla
is the covariant derivative associated to the Christoffel symbols of
the second kind (
1.11
) obtained from the background metric. The Euler-Lagrange equation corresponding to the total Lagrangian density is given
by

[
69
,
75
]

-\frac{1}{2}\left(\nabla_{c}\nabla^{c}\bar{h}_{ab}-\nabla_{a}\nabla^{c}\bar{h}_{cb}-\nabla_{b}\nabla^{c}\bar{h}_{ca}+g_{ab}\nabla^{c}\nabla^{d}\bar{h}_{cd}-2\mathchoice{R^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}\bar{h}_{cd}\right)\\
-\frac{1}{2\alpha}\left[\nabla_{a}\nabla^{c}\bar{h}_{cb}+\nabla_{b}\nabla^{c}\bar{h}_{ca}+\left(1+\frac{1+\beta}{\beta}\right)\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}\bar{h}\right]\\
+\frac{1+\beta}{\alpha\beta}{g}_{ab}\left[\nabla^{c}\nabla^{d}\bar{h}_{cd}+\left(\frac{2(1+\beta)}{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\right)\nabla_{c}\nabla^{c}\bar{h}\right]=\frac{\kappa^{2}}{2}T_{ab},

(3.5)

where

T_{ab}\coloneqq-\frac{2}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\text{matter}}}{\partial g^{ab}}

(3.6)

is the stress-energy tensor computed using only the background metric, and we have used the

trace-reversed perturbation

\displaystyle\bar{h}_{ab}

\displaystyle\coloneqq{h}_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}g_{ab}h,

\displaystyle\text{with }\bar{h}

\displaystyle\coloneqq g^{ab}\bar{h}_{ab}\equiv-h,

(3.7)

to write the field equation. In regions without sources, we can choose the perturbation to be traceless
\bar{h}=0
and transversal
\nabla^{a}\bar{h}_{ab}=0
, then,
Eq. (
3.5
) reduces to the simpler case (we drop the bar in this case as it becomes redundant)

\nabla_{c}\nabla^{c}{h}_{ab}-2\mathchoice{R^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{R^{{{c}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{d}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.57375pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1205pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.51463pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}{b}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.16287pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.55038pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.8217pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{h}_{cd}=0.

(3.8)

These conditions are known as the
Traceless and Transverse gauge
(TT). An additional advantage of this choice of gauge is that the trace reversed
perturbation coincides with the original one
\bar{h}_{ab}\equiv h_{ab}
, so we can drop the bars in the following discussion.

We equivalently obtain the field equations if we consider instead of the full matter Lagrangian the interaction action

I_{\text{int}}[h,T]\equiv\frac{1}{2}\iiiint_{M}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\,\sqrt{-g}\,h_{ab}\,T^{ab},

(3.9)

that couples the gravitational perturbation to the stress-energy tensor.

If we have two solutions of Eq. (
3.5
), namely
h_{ab}^{(1)}
and
h_{ab}^{(2)}
, we can define the generalized
Klein-Gordon inner product between these two by

\langle h^{(1)},h^{(2)}\rangle_{\text{t}}\coloneqq-\mathrm{i}\iiint_{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Sigma_{c}\left[\overline{h_{ab}^{(1)}}\Pi_{(2)}^{cab}-\overline{\Pi_{(1)}^{cab}}h_{ab}^{(2)}\right],

(3.10)

where the overline symbolizes the complex conjugate, while
\Pi^{(1)\,cab}
and
\Pi^{(2)\,cab}
are the generalized momenta

\Pi^{cab}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\partial{\mathcal{L}_{\textrm{p}}}}{\partial{(\nabla_{c}h_{ab})}},

(3.11)

associated to the solutions
h_{ab}^{(1)}
and
h_{ab}^{(2)}
respectively. The generalized Klein-Gordon inner product (
3.10
) is a
conserved symplectic product, which can be seen from the fact that the integrand (in the square bracket) has a null 4-divergence (see also
Refs.
[
60
,
75
]
).

Using the TT gauge, the generalized momentum has the simple form

\Pi^{abc}=-\frac{1}{2\kappa^{2}}\nabla^{a}h^{bc},

(3.12)

and therefore in this gauge it is evident that the normalization and the perturbations are independent of the choice of parameters
\alpha,\beta
, and the
product is given by

\langle h^{(1)},h^{(2)}\rangle_{\text{t}}\coloneqq\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2\kappa^{2}}\iiint_{\Sigma}\mathrm{d}^{3}\Sigma_{c}\left[\overline{h_{ab}^{(1)}}\nabla^{c}h_{(2)}^{ab}-\nabla^{c}\overline{h_{(1)}^{ab}}h_{ab}^{(2)}\right],

(3.13)

very similar to the scalar case.

3.2
Dynamics of the uniformly accelerated mass: the energy-momentum tensor

As we know, the motion of a uniformly accelerated particle with mass
m
in uniform proper constant acceleration
a
along the
z
axis in Minkowski
spacetime is parametrized by the worldline of Eq. (
1.24
): this gives a full account of the kinematics of the
situation. On the other hand, the equation of motion of this system can be obtained rather simply by taking the derivatives regarding proper time

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}t}{\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}}

\displaystyle=a\sinh(a\tau)=a^{2}t,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}z}{\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}}

\displaystyle=a\cosh(a\tau)=a^{2}z,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}x}{\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}y}{\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}}=0,

(3.14)

which can be written in covariant language as (we include the mass of the particle to reaffirm the fact that this is a dynamical equation at heart, and as we
will need it in the future to construct the Lagrangian)

m\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}x^{a}}{\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}}=mF^{a},

(3.15)

where we have defined the 4-vector

F^{b}(x)=a^{2}(t,0,0,z),

(3.16)

which describes the effect of an external forcing agent over the particle.

In the RRW, the newly defined vector can be found using a general coordinate transformation. The (a priori) nonzero components of the forcing vector are given
by

\displaystyle F^{\lambda}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{t}{\lambda})F^{t}+(\partial_{z}{\lambda})F^{z},

\displaystyle F^{\xi}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{t}{\xi})F^{t}+(\partial_{z}{\xi})F^{z},

(3.17)

and as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{z}{a(z^{2}-t^{2})},

\displaystyle\partial_{z}{\lambda}

\displaystyle=-\frac{t}{a(z^{2}-t^{2})},

\displaystyle\partial_{t}{\xi}

\displaystyle=-\frac{t}{a(z^{2}-t^{2})},

\displaystyle\partial_{z}{\xi}

\displaystyle=\frac{z}{a(z^{2}-t^{2})},

(3.18)

we find by simple substitution

\displaystyle F^{\lambda}=\left(\frac{z}{a(z^{2}-t^{2})}\right)(a^{2}t)+\left(-\frac{t}{a(z^{2}-t^{2})}\right)(a^{2}z)=0

(3.19a)

\displaystyle F^{\xi}=\left(-\frac{t}{a(z^{2}-t^{2})}\right)(a^{2}t)+\left(\frac{z}{a(z^{2}-t^{2})}\right)(a^{2}z)=a,

(3.19b)

therefore in Rindler coordinates

F^{b}=(0,a,0,0).

(3.20)

We need to postulate a generalization of the equation of motion, as we know the 4-acceleration does not transform as a vector.

Taking as a starting point the geodesic equation
[
43
]
, considering we must recover the trajectory of a free particle if the acceleration
field is turned off, we can use the new equation of motion

m\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}x^{a}}{\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}}+\Gamma^{a}{}_{bc}\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{b}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{c}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\right)=mF^{a},

(3.21)

where
m
is the rest-mass of the particle. This equation reduces to the geodesic equation followed by a free particle if we turn off the forcing vector
F^{a}
. Let us check if this equation is satisfied by the worldline (
1.25
) when written in Rindler coordinates. From
(
1.21
) we see that equation (
3.21
) reduces to the coupled system of equations
2
2

2

Using the overdot notation to
denote derivatives with respect to proper time.

\displaystyle\ddot{\lambda}+2a\dot{\xi}\dot{\lambda}=0,

(3.22a)

\displaystyle\ddot{\xi}+a(\dot{\xi}^{2}+\dot{\lambda}^{2})=a,

(3.22b)

\displaystyle\ddot{x}=0,

(3.22c)

\displaystyle\ddot{y}=0.

(3.22d)

If we use the
ansatz

\xi=x=y=0
, we see that Eqs. (
3.22c
) and (
3.22d
) are trivially satisfied, while
Eqs. (
3.22a
) and (
3.22b
) imply that
\ddot{\lambda}=0
and
\dot{\lambda}^{2}=1
respectively, which accept the
solution
\lambda=\tau
, and thus, the worldline (
1.24
) is a solution of (
3.22
), which suggests
our Eq. (
3.21
) is an appropriate equation of motion for the uniformly accelerated particle in any (curved) spacetime.

Let us now postulate a Lagrangian that will yield the corresponding equation of motion (
3.21
). Assuming the accelerating agent applies a
conservative force on the particle we can write

L_{\text{p}}(g_{ab},\dot{x}^{a},x^{a},\tau)=-m\sqrt{-g_{ab}\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{a}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{b}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}}-V(g_{ab},F^{a})=L_{\mathrm{k}}-V(g_{ab},F^{a}),

(3.23)

where
L_{\mathrm{k}}
is the kinetic term of the Lagrangian and
V
the interaction potential term between the forcing term
F^{a}
and the particle. Then,
the matter action is

I_{\text{mat}}[g_{ab},\dot{x}^{a},x^{a}]=\int\mathrm{d}\tau\,L_{\text{mat}}\left(g_{ab},\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{a}}{\mathrm{d}\tau},x^{a}\right)\\
=I_{F}-m\int\mathrm{d}\tau\,\sqrt{-g_{ab}\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{a}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{b}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}}-\int\mathrm{d}\tau\,V(g_{ab},F^{a}).

(3.24)

Here the action
I_{F}
describes the dynamics of the forcing field
F^{a}
in and of itself. The condition
\delta{I_{\text{mat}}}=0
yields the
Euler-Lagrange equations for the particle

\frac{\delta{S_{\text{mat}}}}{\delta{x^{a}}}=\frac{\partial{L_{\text{mat}}}}{\partial{x^{a}}}-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\left(\frac{\partial{L_{\text{mat}}}}{\partial{(\mathrm{d}{x}^{a}/\mathrm{d}\tau)}}\right)=0,

(3.25)

which after some algebra can be expressed as

m\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}x^{a}}{\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}}+\Gamma^{a}{}_{bc}\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{b}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{c}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\right)=-g^{ab}\left(\frac{\partial{V}}{\partial{g_{cd}}}\partial_{b}g_{cd}+\frac{\partial{V}}{\partial{F^{c}}}\partial_{b}F^{c}\right),

(3.26)

that can be compared with Eq. (
3.21
) to see that

F^{a}(x)=-\frac{1}{m}g^{ab}\left(\frac{\partial{V}}{\partial{g_{cd}}}\partial_{b}g_{cd}+\frac{\partial{V}}{\partial{F^{c}}}\partial_{b}F^{c}\right).

(3.27)

Naively we can postulate the potential
V=\alpha g_{ab}F^{a}x^{b}
, which in inertial coordinates is given by
V=\alpha a^{2}(t^{2}-z^{2})
, and after applying
it into Eq. (
3.27
) and comparing it with Eq. (
3.16
) we see that
\alpha=m/2
. However, if we write this
V

in Rindler coordinates we obtain
V=-ma\xi\mathrm{e}^{2a\xi}/2
, which does not yield the forcing
F^{a}
found on
(
3.19
) if we compute Eq. (
3.27
) from it. This is because
x^{a}
are not the components of a
4-vector under general coordinate transformations, and this form of Lagrangian is not a scalar. Another possible candidate for our interaction Lagrangian can
be written as

V=-\beta g_{bc}F^{b}F^{c},

(3.28)

which in inertial and Rindler coordinates is simply written as

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\beta a^{4}(t^{2}-z^{2}),

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=-\beta a^{2}\mathrm{e}^{2a\xi},

(3.29)

respectively. Both of these yield the appropriate
F^{a}
after applying them into Eq. (
3.27
) if
\beta=m/(2a^{2})
, therefore

I_{\text{mat}}\left[g,\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}\tau},x,F\right]=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\left(-m\sqrt{-g_{ab}\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{a}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{b}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}}+\frac{m}{2a^{2}}F_{b}(x)F^{b}(x)\right)+I_{F},

(3.30)

will be our full matter action for a mass accelerated for an infinite amount of time including the forcing agent
F^{a}
. This action can be written in terms
of a Lagrangian density by means of the identity

f\left(x,\frac{\mathrm{d}x}{\mathrm{d}\tau},\tau\right)=\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x^{\prime}\,\delta^{4}(x^{\prime}-x)\,f\!\left(x^{\prime},\frac{\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}}{\mathrm{d}\tau},\tau\right),

(3.31)

to see that

I_{\text{mat}}=I_{F}+\iiiint_{\mathbb{R}^{4}}\mathrm{d}^{4}x\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\,\left(-m\sqrt{-g_{ab}\dot{z}^{a}\dot{z}^{b}}+\frac{m}{2a^{2}}F_{b}(z)F^{b}(z)\right)\delta^{4}\bm{(}x-z(\tau)\bm{)}.

(3.32)

We can obtain an energy-momentum density for the infinitely accelerated mass and the accelerating agent by

T^{ab}_{\text{full}}=\frac{2}{\sqrt{-g}}\frac{\delta I_{\text{mat}}}{\delta g_{ab}}=T^{ab}_{F}+\frac{m}{\sqrt{-g}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\left(\dot{z}^{a}\dot{z}^{b}+\frac{1}{a^{2}}F^{a}(z)F^{b}(z)\right)\delta^{4}\bm{(}x-z(\tau)\bm{)},

(3.33)

with
T^{ab}_{F}\coloneqq\delta I_{F}/\delta g_{ab}
. This energy-momentum tensor takes into account the external agent, but our purposes are to describe the
radiation produced by the mass exclusively, therefore it is reasonable to only consider

T^{ab}=\frac{m}{\sqrt{-g}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tau\ \dot{z}^{a}\dot{z}^{b}\,\delta^{4}\bm{(}x-z(\tau)\bm{)},

(3.34)

as our stress-energy tensor, which only includes the influence of the motion of the particle. One important thing to mention is that the stress-energy tensor
we will use is not conserved, however the full energy-momentum tensor defined in Eq. (
3.33
) must be conserved.

We will use the same trajectory of Eq. (
2.15
) to have a compactly supported source. However, it is also convenient
for us to use the description using the proper time of the particle given by

\displaystyle\chi^{t}(\tau)=\begin{cases}-a^{-1}\sinh(aT)+(\tau+T)\cosh(aT),&\text{if }-\Theta_{L}\leq\tau<-T,\\
a^{-1}\sinh(a\tau),&\text{if }-T\leq\tau\leq T,\\
a^{-1}\sinh(aT)+(\tau-T)\cosh(aT),&\text{if }T<\tau\leq\Theta_{L},\end{cases}

(3.35a)

\displaystyle\chi^{x}(\tau)=0,

(3.35b)

\displaystyle\chi^{y}(\tau)=0,

(3.35c)

\displaystyle\chi^{z}(\tau)=\begin{cases}a^{-1}\cosh(aT)-(\tau+T)\sinh(aT)&\text{if }-\Theta_{L}\leq\tau<-T,\\
a^{-1}\cosh(a\tau)&\text{if }-T\leq\tau\leq T,\\
a^{-1}\cosh(aT)+(\tau-T)\sinh(aT)&\text{if }T<\tau\leq\Theta_{L},\end{cases}

(3.35d)

where we used

\Theta_{L}\coloneqq T+\frac{L-a^{-1}\sinh(aT)}{\cosh(aT)},

(3.36)

as an auxiliary function that describes the times of birth (
-\Theta_{L}
) and death (
\Theta_{L}
) of the particle, as registered by an observer commoving with the
particle. And thus the expression we will finally use is

T^{ab}=\frac{m}{\sqrt{-g}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\ \dot{\chi}^{a}\dot{\chi}^{b}\,\delta^{4}\bm{(}x-\chi(\tau)\bm{)}\ \mathrm{d}\tau.

(3.37)

Given how we separated the stress-energy tensor and how the support of the accelerated part has been compactified, we can write the full perturbation as
h_{ab}=h_{ab}^{\mathrm{A}}+h_{ab}^{F}
, where the label
F
indicates the contribution of the accelerating agent and
\mathrm{A}
labels the
accelerated part, which will satisfy in Minkowski spacetime the field equation

\nabla_{c}\nabla^{c}{h}^{\mathrm{A}}_{ab}=-\kappa^{2}\left(T_{ab}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{ab}T\right)\eqcolon-\kappa^{2}\mathfrak{t}_{ab},

(3.38)

where
T^{ab}
is defined as in Eq. (
3.37
) and
T=\eta^{ab}T_{ab}
is the trace of the compactified
stress-energy tensor. As we disregard the action of the accelerating agent, we will drop the super-index A in the rest of the text.

3.3
Gauge-invariant gravitational perturbations and tensor modes

Here we present the method introduced by Kodama, Ishibashi and Seto
[
77
,
78
]
to define
gravitational perturbations by constructing gauge invariant quantities in spacetimes
(M,g_{ab})
of dimension
m+n
, where
M
can be locally written as
the product of an
m
-dimensional Lorentzian manifold
\mathcal{M}
(referred to as the orbit spacetime) and a maximally symmetric
n
-dimensional
Riemannian manifold
\mathcal{N}
:

M=\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{N},

(3.39)

and where the background metric is separable, in the sense that it can be expressed as the sum of two metrics for
\mathcal{M}
and (conformally)
\mathcal{N}
, i.e.

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=g_{ab}\,\mathrm{d}x^{a}\,\mathrm{d}x^{b}=g_{\alpha\beta}(y)\,\mathrm{d}y^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{d}y^{\beta}+r^{2}(y)\,\mathring{g}_{ij}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z^{i}\,\mathrm{d}z^{j},

(3.40)

with
\mathring{g}_{ij}(z)\,\mathrm{d}z^{i}\,\mathrm{d}z^{j}
being the line element in
\mathcal{N}
, which we consider having constant sectional curvature
N
. Here we
used
y
as the coordinates for
\mathcal{M}
and
z
for the ones used to map
\mathcal{N}
, and thus the indices from the beginning of the Greek
alphabet (
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\ldots
) run from 0 to
m-1
, while the indices spanning the middle of the Latin alphabet (
i,j,k,\ldots
) run from
m
to

m+n-1
.
[
79
]

[
79
]
studied
independently the case where the bulk spacetime is maximally symmetric and provides another exposition on the subject. To introduce this formalism we will use,
in this section only and up until the beginning of Sect.
3.3.1
, an
m+n
dimensional spacetime with cosmological constant
\Lambda
and then
adapt it for our purposes .

The main idea behind these papers is to make use of the unique decomposition of the rank 2 tensors in terms of the scalar, vector and tensor sectors, that are
dependent on the respective harmonics defined in the maximally symmetric space
\mathcal{N}
, to then define gauge-independent variables that allow us to
write full gravitational perturbations. The gauge invariants depend on
master variables
for the case the spacetime is vacuum, and the orbit spacetime is
two-dimensional (
m=2
); we briefly present this in the following, however, we stray a little from the notation of the original works in order to be more
consistent with our previous presentation, but also further expand on some details in the discussion.

The metric defined by Eq. (
3.40
) can be written as a block matrix

g_{ab}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}g_{\alpha\beta}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&r^{2}\mathring{g}_{ij}\end{array}\right).

(3.41)

This is useful to see that, as the metric of the orbit spacetime and maximally symmetric space are
g^{\alpha\beta}
and
\mathring{g}^{ij}
respectively,
then it is straightforward to see that the inverse metric of the bulk spacetime is simply

g^{ab}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c|c}g^{\alpha\beta}&0\\
\hline\cr 0&r^{-2}\mathring{g}^{ij}\end{array}\right).

(3.42)

This is used to show that the only nonzero Christoffel symbols of the second kind on the bulk are given by

only holds for
r=1
(or any other constant value really). This can be seen through an example: if
we consider a vector field
A^{a}(x)
defined in the bulk, we have

\nabla_{\alpha}A^{\beta}=\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}A^{\beta},

(3.44a)

\nabla_{i}A^{\alpha}=\partial_{i}A^{\alpha}-\frac{1}{r}A_{i}\mathrm{D}^{\alpha}r,

(3.44b)

\nabla_{\alpha}A^{i}=\partial_{\alpha}A^{i}+\frac{1}{r}A^{i}\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}r,

(3.44c)

\nabla_{i}A^{j}=\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{i}A^{j}+\frac{1}{r}\delta^{i}_{j}A^{\alpha}\,\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}r,

(3.44d)

and thus the covariant derivatives have to be carefully computed when necessary.

Tensor sector perturbation

A harmonic tensor field
\mathbb{T}_{ij}(z)
in
\mathcal{N}
is a solution of the equation:

(\mathring{\mathrm{D}}^{k}\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{k}+\sigma^{2})\mathbb{T}_{ij}=0,

(3.45)

where
\sigma^{2}
is an eigenvalue. We impose that the harmonic tensor is traceless and transverse:

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathbb{T}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\mathbb{T}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\mathbb{T}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\mathbb{T}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathring{\mathrm{D}}^{j}\mathbb{T}_{ij}

\displaystyle=0.

(3.46)

As the operator
\mathring{\mathrm{D}}^{k}\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{k}
should be self-adjoint,
\sigma^{2}
is non-negative, and we also discard
\sigma=0
as it produces
constant tensors and correspond with a zero-measure set in the solution space.
The full metric perturbation for the tensor sector on the bulk manifold
M
is given by

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{(t)}}_{\alpha\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{(t)}}_{\alpha i}(x)

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle h^{\mathrm{(t)}}_{ij}(x)

\displaystyle=2r^{2}\Omega_{\mathrm{t}}(y)\mathbb{T}_{ij}(z),

(3.47)

where the scalar function
\Omega_{\mathrm{t}}
is a solution of the equation

\mathrm{D}^{\alpha}\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}\Omega_{\mathrm{t}}+\frac{n}{r}(\mathrm{D}^{\alpha}r)\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}\Omega_{\mathrm{t}}-\frac{\sigma^{2}+2N}{r^{2}}\Omega_{\mathrm{t}}=0,

(3.48)

where the right-hand side is zero as the vacuum is not affected by a gauge choice. With this, alongside normalization and physical conditions of the system, the
full metric perturbation for the tensor sector on the bulk is completely determined.

Vector sector perturbation

All harmonic divergence-free vector fields in
\mathcal{N}
are defined by the equations

\displaystyle(\mathring{\mathrm{D}}^{k}\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{k}+\sigma^{2})\mathbb{V}_{i}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathring{\mathrm{D}}^{i}\mathbb{V}_{i}

\displaystyle=0.

(3.49)

From where we can define the traceless tensor

\mathbb{V}_{ij}\coloneqq-\frac{1}{2\sigma}\left(\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{i}\mathbb{V}_{j}+\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{j}\mathbb{V}_{i}\right),

(3.50)

which satisfies

\displaystyle[\mathring{\mathrm{D}}^{k}\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{k}+\sigma^{2}-(n+1)N]\mathbb{V}_{ij}=0,

(3.51a)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathbb{V}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\mathbb{V}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\mathbb{V}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\mathbb{V}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}=0,

(3.51b)

\displaystyle\mathring{\mathrm{D}}^{j}\mathbb{V}_{ij}=\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}-(n-1)N}{2\sigma}\right)\mathbb{V}_{i}.

(3.51c)

The vector harmonics expand
the vector sector
of the full metric perturbation as

\displaystyle h^{(\mathrm{v})}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle h^{(\mathrm{v})}_{\alpha i}

\displaystyle=rf_{\alpha}(y)\mathbb{V}_{i}(z),

\displaystyle h^{(\mathrm{v})}_{ij}

\displaystyle=2r^{2}\Omega_{\mathrm{t}}(y)\mathbb{V}_{ij}(z).

(3.52)

Here
\Omega_{\mathrm{t}}
is defined by Eq. (
3.48
), and the vector gauge-dependent field
f_{\alpha}
can be
constructed introducing the
vector-sector master variable

\Omega_{\mathrm{v}}(y)
and the gauge-invariant
F_{\alpha}(y)
by

F_{\alpha}(y)\coloneqq f_{\alpha}(y)+\frac{r}{\sigma}\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}\Omega_{\textrm{t}}(y)=\frac{1}{r^{n-1}}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}\mathrm{D}^{\beta}\Omega_{\mathrm{v}}(y),

(3.53)

where
\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}
is the 2-dimensional totally antisymmetric Levi-Civita
pseudo-tensor
3
3

3

In the paper they use the version with
contravariant indices, but don’t explain whether it is the Levi-Civita symbol (which takes values of
-1
, 0, or 1) or the tensor version of it (multiplied by

\sqrt{-g}
). We assume they are using the tensor version as
f^{\alpha}
must be a tensor, not a density, and thus, raise and lower indices only using the
metric and its inverse.
defined in
{M}
, and the master variable’s dynamics are governed by the equation

\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}\mathrm{D}^{\alpha}\Omega_{\mathrm{v}}-\frac{n}{r}(\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}r)\mathrm{D}^{\alpha}\Omega_{\mathrm{v}}-\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}-(n-1)N}{r^{2}}\right)\Omega_{\mathrm{v}}=\frac{C}{r^{2}},

(3.54)

where the constant
C
of the right hand side can be set to zero by redefining
\Omega_{\mathrm{v}}
(i.e., relocalizing this quantity).

Scalar sector perturbation

The scalar harmonic field
\mathbb{S}(z)
in
\mathcal{N}
satisfies the equation

\displaystyle(\mathring{\mathrm{D}}^{k}\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{k}+\sigma^{2})\mathbb{S}

\displaystyle=0,

(3.55)

and this allows us to define the scalar-type harmonic vector

\mathbb{S}_{i}\coloneqq-\frac{1}{\sigma}\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{i}\mathbb{S},

(3.56)

satisfying

\displaystyle[\mathring{\mathrm{D}}^{k}\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{k}+\sigma^{2}-(n-1)N]\mathbb{S}_{i}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{i}\mathbb{S}^{i}

\displaystyle=\sigma\mathbb{S}.

(3.57)

From this it is also possible to define the scalar-type harmonic tensors in
\mathcal{N}
as

\mathbb{S}_{ij}\coloneqq\frac{1}{\sigma^{2}}\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{i}\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{j}\mathbb{S}+\frac{1}{n}\mathring{g}_{ij}\mathbb{S},

(3.58)

with the properties

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathbb{S}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\mathbb{S}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\mathbb{S}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}{\mathbb{S}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{i}}}}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{j}\mathchoice{\mathbb{S}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathbb{S}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathbb{S}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}{\mathbb{S}^{{{i}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.71356pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.29834pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.64166pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[2.82928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[1.68811pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.2058pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{j}}}}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n-1}{n}\right)\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}-nN}{\sigma}\right)\mathbb{S}_{i},

\displaystyle\left(\mathring{\mathrm{D}}^{k}\mathring{\mathrm{D}}_{j}+\sigma^{2}-2nN\right)\mathbb{S}_{ij}=0.

(3.59)

The full perturbation is given by

\displaystyle h^{(\mathrm{s})}_{\alpha\beta}(y,z)=f_{\alpha\beta}(y)\mathbb{S}(z),

(3.60a)

\displaystyle h^{(\mathrm{s})}_{\alpha i}=r(y)f_{\alpha}(y)\mathbb{S}_{i}(z),

(3.60b)

\displaystyle h^{(\mathrm{s})}_{ij}=2r^{2}(y)[\mathring{g}_{ij}\Omega_{\mathrm{l}}(y)\mathbb{S}(z)+\Omega_{\mathrm{t}}(y)\mathbb{S}_{ij}(z)].

(3.60c)

For modes satisfying
\sigma^{2}(\sigma^{2}-nN)\neq 0
(we will disregard the modes that do not satisfy this condition for eigenvalues), we can define the
gauge invariants

\displaystyle F(y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\Omega_{\mathrm{l}}(y)+\frac{1}{n}\Omega_{\mathrm{t}}(y)+X^{\alpha}(y)\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}r(y),

\displaystyle F_{\alpha\beta}(y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq f_{\alpha\beta}(y)+2\mathrm{D}_{(\alpha}X_{\beta)}(y),

(3.61)

with the auxiliary vector

X_{\alpha}(y)\coloneqq\frac{r(y)}{\sigma}\left(f_{\alpha}(y)+\frac{r(y)}{\sigma}\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}\Omega_{\mathrm{t}}(y)\right)=\frac{r(y)}{\sigma}F_{\alpha}(y),

(3.62)

to describe the gauge-invariant part of the perturbation, which can be written in terms of a
scalar master variable

\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}
as

\displaystyle r^{n-2}F_{\alpha\beta}=\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}\mathrm{D}_{\beta}\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}-\frac{n-1}{n}[\mathrm{D}^{\gamma}\mathrm{D}_{\gamma}\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}]g_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{2(n-2)\Lambda}{n^{2}(n+1)}\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}g_{\alpha\beta},

(3.63)

\displaystyle r^{n-2}F=\frac{1}{2n}\left[\mathrm{D}^{\gamma}\mathrm{D}_{\gamma}\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}+\frac{4\Lambda}{n(n+1)}\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}\right],

(3.64)

which, in turn, is governed by the field equation

\mathrm{D}^{\alpha}\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}(y)-\frac{n}{r}[\mathrm{D}^{\alpha}r(y)]\mathrm{D}_{\alpha}\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}(y)-\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}-nN}{r^{2}(y)}+\frac{2(n-2)\Lambda}{n(n+1)}\right)\Omega_{\mathrm{s}}(y)=0.

(3.65)

Notice how the cosmological constant only couples with this mode.

We apply this formalism to Rindler and Minkowski spacetimes to define gravitational modes.

3.3.1
Harmonics on the plane and application to Rindler spacetime

Rindler spacetime can be identified as the manifold
\mathbb{R}^{4}=\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}
with the metric of Eq. (
1.20a
).
Here, we can recognize that using the language of the previous section:
m=n=2
,
g_{\alpha\beta}=\diag(-\mathrm{e}^{2a\xi},\mathrm{e}^{2a\xi})
,
r(\lambda,\xi)=1
,
\mathring{g}_{ij}={g}_{ij}=\diag(+1,+1)
, and
N=0
, to make use of the master variable version of the aforementioned formalism. We
remember that for the orbit spacetime
\mathbb{R}^{2}
[described by the coordinates
(\lambda,\xi)
] the nonzero components of the Levi-Civita pseudo-tensor
are given by
\epsilon_{\lambda\xi}=-\epsilon_{\xi\lambda}=\mathrm{e}^{2a\xi}
, and that it is covariantly constant, i.e.
\mathrm{D}_{\gamma}\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}=0
.

Moreover, on this spacetime we have
\mathchoice{\Gamma^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53473pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53473pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80048pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.00034pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{x}{b}}}}{\Gamma^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53473pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53473pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80048pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.00034pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{x}{b}}}}{\Gamma^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53473pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53473pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80048pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.00034pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{x}{b}}}}{\Gamma^{{{a}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53473pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53473pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.80048pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.00034pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox
